Accountability Indicator Descriptions and Variance Commentary:

This accountability indicator measures engagement that the ACT has with the Commonwealth Grants Commission (CGC) on the distribution of the Goods and Services Tax (GST) grant. The provision of submissions to the CGC is not discretionary as data from the ACT is essential for the accurate determination of the GST relativities, and the zero-sum nature of the GST distribution means that the ACT must represent its interests on issues relating to the distribution method or risk a reduced GST share. For 2025-26, the indicator is based on (1) a written response to the CGC’s revised Draft Report Justice assessment chapter (CGC’s Draft Report); (2) a written response to the CGC’s Overview of final justice assessment paper; (3) data returns for the 2026-27 GST relativities; and (4) a written response to CGC’s New Issues for the 2026-27 GST relativities. For 2026-27, the indicator is based on (1) data returns for the 2027-28 GST relativities and (2) a written response to CGC’s New Issues for the 2027-28 GST relativities.

This accountability indicator covers briefings prepared on the most recent key economic indicators for Canberra and the ACT based on Australian Bureau of Statistics’ (ABS) data releases and changes to the RBA cash rate target. The number of ABS data releases of relevance to the ACT’s economic circumstances and RBA cash rate changes determine the frequency and number of economic indicators produced. The objective of these briefings is to contextualise the information from the ABS and RBA releases for the ACT community and the ACT Government. The briefing notes also inform analysis on the outlook for the ACT and national economies, and updates to economic forecasts prepared through the budget process. The latest briefings are available on the ACT Treasury website. For 2025-26, the ABS changed the release frequency for Consumer Price Index from quarterly to monthly, which largely accounts for the difference between the 2025-25 target and estimated outcome. For 2026-27, the indicator is based on the expected number of briefings for ABS data releases.

This indicator covers responsibility for ACT Treasury input to the operations of the Council on Federal Financial Relations (CFFR), the Board of Treasurers (the Board) and Heads of Treasuries. The timing and frequency of these meetings can change as this is determined by the respective Chair. For 2026-27, the meeting frequency is based on four meetings per annum of each intergovernmental forum.

This indicator measures the advice provided on Tier 1 infrastructure capital works projects being developed under the Capital Framework. The Capital Framework provides guidance to directorates and agencies on the development of infrastructure capital works projects, to ensure that business cases for these projects provide a robust basis for Government to invest in them, and for them to proceed to procurement and delivery. Tier 1 projects are infrastructure capital works projects assessed as Tier 1 under the Capital Framework. This is having an estimated project capital cost of $100 million or above, or having a project cost of $25 million to $100 million and considered high risk.

This indicator measures the efficiency of Infrastructure and Commercial Advice (ICA) in undertaking its responsibilities for Life of Project transactions for Public Private Partnerships (PPP) projects. PPPs are a form of infrastructure procurement that involve high levels of risk transfer to the private sector and high levels of integrated delivery. ‘Life of Project’ transactions are those that have a material impact on the financial and/or operational arrangements of the project as negotiated in the original PPP Contract, and normally result from a request by the Project Company that requires the consent of the Territory (for example, debt refinancing, changes in equity arrangements, changes to the payment mechanism or financial model, etc). ‘Successfully executed’ means that the Territory has responded to a request from the Project Company within the timescale specified in the PPP Contract. The full year result is the number of Life of Project transactions completed successfully divided by the total number of such transactions scheduled to be completed within the reporting period.

This indicator measures the efficiency of ICA in undertaking its responsibilities for managing the assessment of Unsolicited Proposals. The Guidelines for Unsolicited Proposals is the framework for the treatment of Unsolicited Proposals in the Territory. The relevant timescales are those specified in the Guidelines and/or those agreed with the proponent, for each phase of the assessment process. Within three months of receiving the formal Concept Submission, the Government will aim to advise the Proponent whether the Unsolicited Proposals Steering Committee (UPSC) would like to receive a presentation. Within two months of the presentation to the UPSC, the UPSC will notify the Proponent of its decision on progressing the proposal, unless there are particular circumstances that require a longer review period. The full year result is the number of responses to proponents within the specified timescale divided by the total number of responses. The 2025-26 estimated outcome is not applicable (n/a) as no unsolicited proposals were due for processing between 1 July 2025 and 2 March 2026.

Discontinued accountability indicator. Housing targets are published each year as part of the Indicative Land release Program (ILRP) published with the Budget and include land released for public, community and affordable housing. Section 65 of the City Renewal Authority and Suburban Land Agency Act 2017 requires relevant Ministers to determine the public, community and affordable housing targets for land release sites in the ILRP. This accountability indicator has been discontinued in 2026-27 as the Administrative Arrangements Order 2025 (2) (NI2025-686), dated 19 December 2025, transferred responsibility for the functions of affordable and community housing policy, and housing strategy and policy, from CMTEDD to City and Environment Directorate.

Discontinued accountability indicator. The ACT Housing Strategy commits to annual reporting on the progress of actions in its complementary Implementation Plan. This commitment is realised through the preparation of an annual report card made publicly available on the Housing Strategy page of the ACT Government’s Homes and Housing website. This accountability indicator has been discontinued in 2026-27 as the Administrative Arrangements Order 2025 (2) (NI2025-686), dated 19 December 2025, transferred responsibility for the functions of affordable and community housing policy, and housing strategy and policy, from CMTEDD to City and Environment Directorate.

Accountability Indicator Descriptions and Variance Commentary:

The Territory Budget is presented to the Legislative Assembly in accordance with the timing prescribed in section 5 of the Financial Management Act 1996 (FMA).

The Budget Review for the Territory is presented to the Legislative Assembly in accordance with the timing prescribed in section 20A of the FMA.

The Annual Financial Statements for the Territory are provided to the Auditor General in accordance with the timing prescribed in section 24 of the FMA.

The Quarterly Consolidated Financial Statements are presented to the Legislative Assembly in accordance with the timing prescribed in section 26 of the FMA.

This accountability indicator covers policy and project initiatives targeted at improving the effectiveness and efficiency of regulation in relation to financial processes.

This accountability indicator covers the annual budget presentation to the credit rating agency S&P Global Ratings. This supports S&P Global Ratings in its assessment of the credit rating of the Australian Capital Territory. The presentation to S&P Global Ratings comprises a selection of content from the Annual Budget Papers.

This accountability indicator covers the submission of Business Activity Statements (BAS) to the Australian Taxation Office (ATO) by the 21st of every month. The date of BAS lodgements processed through the ATO Business Portal and that of GST remittance are monitored and recorded by the action officer.

This accountability indicator covers the lodgement of annual Fringe Benefit Tax returns to the ATO. This is an annual measure.

This accountability indicator refers to the completion of processing of monthly financial information using Oracle Government Financials. Meeting this timeframe (6th working day of the month) facilitates agencies in finalising monthly management reports in a timely manner.

This accountability indicator covers the submission of draft annual financial reports to the directorates/agencies. Meeting this timeframe (10th working day of July) facilitates agencies in finalising the annual financial statements to meet audit office deadlines.

This accountability indicator covers the service requests logged by the Finance Service Desk which are resolved within agreed timeframes (five working days).

Accountability Indicator Descriptions and Variance Commentary:

This accountability indicator measures the level of overdue collectable rates debt as a percentage of forecast rates revenue. It excludes matters subject to objection and appeals. The variance of the 2025-26 estimated outcome to the 2025-26 target is due to increased rates debt due to ongoing effects of legacy debts from the COVID-19 pandemic period when there was a reduction in debt recovery action. The level of overdue debt has since stabilised, and additional effort is being directed to reduce rates debts. The amended 2026-27 Target recognises the persistent nature of the rates debt since COVID-19 and aligns with rates targets for surrounding councils.

This accountability indicator measures the level of overdue collectable debt (less rates) as a percentage of forecast tax revenue (less rates). It excludes matters subject to objection and appeals, under liquidation and agreements made by the ACT Government in respect of pending waivers. The variance of the 2025-26 estimated outcome to the 2025-26 target is due to increased outstanding debt due to ongoing effects of legacy debts from the COVID-19 pandemic period when there was a reduction in debt recovery action. There is now a focus on reducing payroll tax and duty debts. The amended 2026-27 Target recognises the persistent nature of the debt since COVID-19.

Accountability indicator on the completion within timeframes of internal reviews of objections lodged against revenue assessments and decisions. The variance of the 2025-26 estimated outcome to the 2025-26 target is due to high objection and appeal numbers for the last three years. In addition, the Revenue Office is responding to complex tax arrangements and appeals that require more time and resourcing. While the number of matters completed has increased, this has been outweighed by increased volume and complexity.

Accountability indicator on the completion within timeframes of internal reviews of objections lodged against revenue assessments and decisions. The variance of the 2025-26 estimated outcome to the 2025-26 target is due to high objection and appeal numbers for the last three years. In addition, the Revenue Office is responding to complex tax arrangements and appeals that require more time and resourcing. While the number of matters completed has increased, this has been outweighed by increased volume and complexity.

This accountability indicator measures all revenue assessed from compliance activities divided by the number of full-time equivalent inspectors. It includes revenue from assessments and reassessments (being that portion not already assessed), outstanding returns, savings resulting from reductions in refund claims and other compliance activity. The variance of the 2025-26 estimated outcome to the 2025-26 target reflects high levels of compliance activity across a number of tax lines, especially payroll tax.

Accountability Indicator Descriptions and Variance Commentary:

Public availability of the Notifiable Contract Register and Tenders ACT measures the proportion of time that these systems are fully available for use by external parties interested in business opportunities of the ACT Government. This indicator is measured annually using the data provided by the external host of Tenders ACT.

Public satisfaction with Tender systems measures the proportion of external users who are satisfied that tender process and documentation as delivered via Tenders ACT support meeting their business needs and objectives. This is collected annually by a survey of external Tenders ACT register parties.

This indicator measures the effectiveness of procurement training delivered to Territory entity staff. The measure will be calculated based on the percentage of Territory entity staff, who through surveys undertaken following training sessions, have identified as ‘strongly agree’ or ‘agree’ that the training was useful.

Procurement ACT Service Desk releases tender documentation on behalf of Territory entities. This indicator is measured to ensure the required turnaround time so that market participants receive the tender documentation by the planned release date.

<a id="notes-to-the-controlled-budget-statements"></a>

#### Notes to the Controlled Budget Statements

Significant variations are as follows:


<a id="operating-statement"></a>

###### Operating Statement

controlled recurrent payments (CRP): the decrease of $89.866 million in the 2025‑26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget is primarily due to transfer of Affordable Housing Policy Coordination Branch functions to City and Environment Directorate, policy changes to how financial assistance is provided to increase affordable housing supply (e.g. replacing CRP funded grants payment with Waivers for lease variation fee assistance) and the reprofiling of funding into 2026-27 and future years. This was partially offset by appropriation provided for new initiatives as part of the 2026-27 Budget and rollover of unspent funding from last financial year into 2025-26.

employee expenses: the increase of $18.646 million in employee expenses in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget is mainly due to the reclassification of other expenditure types to employee expenses to better reflect the nature of this expenditure.

grants and purchased services:

the decrease of $100.830 million in grants and purchased services expenses in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget is also primarily due to transfer of Affordable Housing Policy Coordination Branch functions to City and Environment Directorate, policy changes to how financial assistance is provided to increase affordable housing supply (e.g. replacing grants payment with Waivers for lease variation fee assistance) and the reprofiling of funding into 2026-27 and future years.

the increase of $20.798 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is primarily due to new initiatives funded as part of the 2026-27 Budget.


<a id="balance-sheet"></a>

###### Balance Sheet

cash and cash equivalents: the increase of $33.649 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget is mainly due to the timing of cash flows towards the end of financial year.

receivables (non-current): the increase of $16.770 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to the timing of and forecast increase in loans to be issued through the Sustainable Household Scheme following Government decision to expand the program.

property, plant and equipment: the reduction of $170.932 million in 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget is mainly due to technical adjustments made to align the budget with the actual asset base, following audited outcome and earlier transfer of assets to other directorates as part of the Machinery of Government changes.

payable: the increase of $28.551 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget is mainly due to the timing of payable liabilities towards the end of financial year.


<a id="statement-of-changes-in-equity-and-cash-flow-statement"></a>

###### Statement of Changes in Equity and Cash Flow Statement

Variations in these Statements are explained in the notes above.

<a id="notes-to-the-territorial-budget-statements"></a>

#### Notes to the Territorial Budget Statements

The Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate (CMTEDD) collects the majority of General Government Sector (GGS) revenue through its Territorial accounts on behalf of the ACT Government. 2026-27 Budget Outlook Paper provides detailed information on budgeted General Government Sector (GGS) revenue, including taxes, Commonwealth Government grants and dividend revenue.

CMTEDD is responsible for payments on behalf of the Territory associated with a range of rebates and concession schemes covering general rates, household utility expenses, transportation, spectacles and funeral expenses. A Payment for Expenses on Behalf of the Territory is received as income to cover these payments.

Significant variations are as follows:


<a id="statement-of-income-and-expenses-on-behalf-of-the-territory"></a>

##### Statement of Income and Expenses on behalf of the Territory

payment of expenses on behalf of the Territory (EBT): the decrease of $23.891 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to the cessation of payments associated with the Energy Bill Relief Program in 2025-26.

taxes, licences, fees and fines receipts: the increase of $303.162 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to growth in Payroll Tax, General Rates, Lease Variation Charge, Conveyance Duty, Fire and Emergency Services Levy, Land Tax and Transfer of Motor Vehicle Registration Duty.

sales of goods and services from contracts with customers: the decrease of $1.293 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to the reduction in rental revenue coming from the Land Rent Scheme.

dividend revenue: the decrease of $78.005 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget is due to decreased dividends from the Suburban Land Agency (SLA), City Renewal Authority (CRA) and Icon Water, and the increase of $55.083 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is due to increased dividends from Icon Water and CRA in that year.

other income: the decrease of $23.074 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget and the increase of $15.967 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is due to the timing of the commencement of the profit share from the operation of the Big Canberra Battery.

grants and purchased services: the decrease of $32.619 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to the cessation of the Energy Bill Relief Program.

other expenses: the increase of $4.841 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025‑26 estimated outcome is mainly due to an agreement with Commonwealth to help fund a reduction in fuel excise, in response to higher fuel prices, partially offset by reduced waivers and reduced loss on disposal of assets.


<a id="statement-of-assets-and-liabilities-on-behalf-of-the-territory"></a>

##### Statement of Assets and Liabilities on behalf of the Territory

receivables (current):

the decrease of $98.439 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget is mainly due to reduced trade receivables, and receivables from SLA.

the increase of $118.829 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to increased trade receivables and increased accrued revenue.

other investments: the increase of $9.335 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to the re-profiling of funding for the investment into the Boosting business and the economy – Knowledge Capital – Future Jobs Fund initiative.

property, plant and equipment: the decrease of $21.565 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is due to the forecast sale of land rent blocks associated with the Land Rent Scheme.

payables (current): the decrease of $92.652 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget, and the increase of $115.311 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025‑26 estimated outcome, largely reflect the movements in receivables explained above, as most funds received through the territorial accounts are on-passed to the Territory Banking Account (TBA).


<a id="statement-of-changes-in-equity-and-cash-flow-statement-on-behalf-of-th"></a>

##### Statement of Changes in Equity and Cash Flow Statement on behalf of the Territory

Variations in these Statements are explained in the notes above.

ACT INSURANCE AUTHORITY – STATEMENT OF INTENT

The ACT Insurance Authority is a Territory Authority established under the Insurance Authority Act 2005 (the Act).

The Statement of Intent for 2026­27 has been prepared in accordance with section 61 of the Financial Management Act 1996 (FMA).

The responsible Minister was consulted during the preparation of the Statement of Intent.

The Statement of Intent, which focuses on the 2026-27 Budget year, has been developed in the context of a four year forward planning horizon to be incorporated, as far as practicable, into the ACT Insurance Authority strategic and business planning processes.

The ACT Insurance Authority 2026-27 Statement of Intent has been agreed between:


|   |   |   |
|---|---|---|
| Russ CampbellUnder Treasurer |   | Alex McPhersonA/g General ManagerACT Insurance Authority |


|   |   |   |
|---|---|---|
| Rachel Stephen-Smith MLAMinister for Finance |   | Chris Steel MLATreasurer |

This page deliberately left blank


<a id="act-insurance-authority"></a>

## ACT INSURANCE AUTHORITY


<a id="purpose-2"></a>

### Purpose


<a id="the-organisation"></a>

#### The Organisation

The ACT Insurance Authority (the Authority) is established under the Insurance Authority Act 2005.

The Authority works to protect the assets and services of the Territory by providing risk management support and insurance services to all ACT Government directorates and statutory authorities. The Authority meets the insurable claims and losses of the ACT Government.

The Authority operates as the ACT Government’s captive insurer of Territory risks. The captive insurance model protects the ACT Government budget from a range of catastrophic and accumulated risk exposures through its insurance arrangements, and the accumulation of a fund reserve to meet the cost of asset losses and legal liabilities that occur as a result of the activities of Government.

The portfolio represents $40.6 billion in insured assets, with estimated revenue of $109.1 million, an operating result of $7.3 million and an equity position of $137.4 million in the 2026-27 Budget.

The Authority reports to the Minister for Finance and the Treasurer through the Under Treasurer, Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate (CMTEDD).

The Authority operates on a cost recovery basis by collecting premiums from directorates and statutory authorities to meet the anticipated costs of claims.


<a id="services-and-functions"></a>

#### Services and Functions

The Authority provides insurance, claims and risk management services to directorates and statutory authorities.

The Authority’s functions are to:

carry on the business of insurer of territory risks;

take out insurance of Territory risks with other entities;

manage and settle claims in relation to territory risks;

develop and promote good practices for the management of territory risks; and

give advice to the Minister about insurance and the management of territory risks.

The Authority’s operational model focuses on satisfying these functions by taking a leadership role with the aim of reducing the total cost of risk to the Territory and individual agencies.

The insurance coverage provided to directorates and statutory authorities and the Authority’s reinsurance program is broad form cover that includes:

public liability;

medical negligence;

professional indemnity;

property damage;

directors and officers’ liability;

standing timber; and

financial crime.

The Authority also arranges insurance policies on behalf of ACT Government agencies to cover contract works, travel, art and galleries, fleet vehicle, aviation, and personal accident cover for both aero retrieval and volunteer workers.  The Authority bears no risk on these policies.


<a id="nature-and-scope-of-activities"></a>

### Nature and Scope of Activities


<a id="general-activities"></a>

#### General Activities

The general activities the Authority intends to undertake in achieving its functions are to:

provide professional advice to the ACT Government and territory agencies on insurance and risk management issues;

deliver a value for money reinsurance program to protect the Territory’s budget;

continue to maximise reinsurance recoveries where feasible;

review the Territory’s asset register as part of the insurance renewal process;

develop business practices which will enable the Authority to achieve best practice results, and if feasible, reduced premiums for agencies;

proactively manage claims against the Territory in consultation with agency stakeholders and in accordance with the ACT model litigant requirements;

conduct regular reviews of existing claims to ensure that appropriate management is being applied and that realistic claim estimates are included in financial statements;

facilitate agency access to claims reporting and data analysis to support a risk managed approach to operational and asset management;

continue to assist agencies with the application of the ACT Government Risk Management Policy;

work with agencies to reduce the number and severity of incidents and ultimate claims cost;

deliver to agencies a program of general and targeted risk management training;

manage the Builders Warranty Insurance scheme on behalf of the Territory through an agreement with QBE to provide Builders Warranty Insurance in the ACT, with the Territory indemnifying QBE in respect of any claim losses;

manage Physical and Sexual Abuse liability (PSA) for out of home care service providers in the ACT, in coordination with the Health and Community Services Directorate;

administer the Office of the Nominal Defendant of the ACT (NOD); and

administer the Default Insurance Fund (DIF).


<a id="risk-management"></a>

#### Risk Management

The Authority has developed and implemented a risk management plan in accordance with the Australian Standard on risk management, AS ISO 31000:2018 and the ACT Government’s Risk Management Policy. The Authority’s plan identifies and details risks, control measures and treatment action plans for financial, business and information technology risks.

The Authority has identified the following key financial risks.

Insufficient and/or unsatisfactory external insurance arrangements.

Annual premiums are not sufficient to fully fund claims over the claim development period.

To manage these risks, the Authority engages a specialised insurance broker to provide professional advice and access to international and local reinsurance markets.  Professional external actuaries provide support and advice that aligns agency premiums with claims experience.


<a id="2026-27-priorities-and-next-three-financial-years"></a>

### 2026-27 Priorities and Next Three Financial Years

Strategic and operational priorities to be pursued in 2026-27 and for the following three financial years include:

implementing a program of reinsurance to protect the Territory budget based on an appropriate balance between transferred and retained risk;

actively managing claims against the Territory in consultation with agency stakeholders and in accordance with the ACT model litigant requirements;

assisting agencies with the implementation and continuous improvement of risk management practices within the Territory that reflect international standards and business best practice;

delivering risk management services including training, targeted educational seminars and consultancy services for agencies that increase the level of stakeholder engagement; and

enhancing the insurance, risk, and claims information management system to enable the Authority to continue to deliver mature services to Territory agencies.

Accountability Indicators


**Table 3: Key Performance Indicators**

The table should be read in conjunction with the accompanying notes.


| Key performance indicator | 2025-26Targets | 2025-26Estimated Outcome | 2026-27Targets |
|---|---|---|---|
| Overall customer satisfaction with the Authority from annual customer satisfaction survey | 80% | 80% | 80% |
| Maintain the Authority’s funding ratio as outlined in the Authority’s Capital Management Plan | 110% | 120% | 110% |
| General and administrative expense as a percentage of total annual premium revenue | 10% | 7% | 10% |
| The average number of days to process payments for the settlement of claims from the day all required documents are received from the agency | 14 days | 22 days | 14 days |
| Complete Property Loss Control Surveys at a number of selected territory locations | 8 | 8 | 8 |
| Hold quarterly reviews of all liability and medical negligence claims to assess the claim management strategy for matters where the territory’s reserve exceeds $250,000 | 4 | 4 | 4 |
| Deliver a program of general and targeted risk management training courses to territory agencies | 48 | 45 | n/a |
| Overall participant satisfaction with risk management training sessions delivered to agency staff members | 90% | 89% | n/a |
| Provide quarterly reporting to directorates to assist the identification, assessment and treatment of risks | 4 | 4 | 4 |

Notes for the key performance indicators:

Surveys are sent to a range of agency contacts including Directors-General, Chief Executive Officers and other key stakeholders of ACT Government directorates and statutory authorities insured by the Authority.  Respondents are asked to rate their overall satisfaction with the Authority’s services based on their experiences over the past 12 months. Satisfaction levels are determined by the respondents selecting either very good, good, average, poor or very poor.  Very good, good and average responses are taken as a positive result.

The funding ratio is calculated by dividing total assets by total liabilities. The Authority aims to maintain its capital position between 100-120 per cent as outlined in the Authority’s capital management plan. A capital position outside this range requires the Authority to consider corrective action. The parameters outlined in the Authority’s capital management plan guide decision making to address a capital position outside the targeted ratio range. This would include action to seek capital injections (in a deficit situation) or returning excess capital (in a surplus situation) to the ACT Government.

The Authority’s general and administrative expenses, including employee expenses, and supplies and services, is calculated as a percentage of gross earned premiums expressed as Sale of Goods and Services from contracts with customers in the operating statement and is measured against the budgeted results for the Authority.

The Authority generally processes payments for the settlement of claims on a fortnightly basis.  The average number of days to process a payment is measured from the date all required documentation relating to settlements is received by the Authority, to the date payment is made.

The Authority’s property reinsurers conduct an annual property loss control survey program on selected Territory locations. Property loss control reports identify the potential for property loss and assist agencies to reduce the risks of loss through loss prevention efforts.  Recommendations are communicated to surveyed Territory agencies for consideration.

The Authority conducts quarterly claims review meetings to review all public liability and medical negligence claims where the Territory’s reserve exceeds $250,000. Meetings are also attended by representatives of the ACT Government Solicitor, the Authority’s insurance brokers, external insurers and their solicitors.

The Authority delivers a program of face-to face risk management training that covers general introductory and intermediate-to-advanced level risk management, along with entity specific training tailored to meet agency requirements. (From 1 July 2026 this performance indicator has been changed to: Attendance at risk training courses, workshops and Community of Practice events, and completion of E-Learning by ACT Government employees. The proposed target for 2026-27 is 1,600. As a result, Table 3 shows the 2026-27 target against the existing indicator as n/a.)

Attendees of risk management training sessions are requested to complete feedback forms at the completion of the courses delivered by the Authority. Attendees are asked to assess the course based on areas such as course suitability, facilitators’ knowledge and whether they would recommend the training. The satisfaction levels are determined by the respondents selecting either strongly agree, agree, disagree or strongly disagree. Strongly agree and agree are taken as a satisfied result. (From 1 July 2026 this performance indicator has been changed to: Provision of risk training and advisory services that promote good risk practices broadly across the ACT Government, with at least 3 risk engagement activities to each Directorate, and at least 1 to each Independent Statutory Authority (ISA), annually. The proposed target for 2026-27 is 80 per cent. As a result, Table 3 shows the 2026-27 target against the existing indicator as n/a.)

The Authority provides quarterly reports to directorates to assist in the identification, assessment, and treatment of risk for Territory activities. The reports provide a range of information and data, including claim numbers, cost of claims and analytics to support ongoing risk management considerations.

Explanation of Variances (+/-10 per cent) Between 2025-26 Target and 2025-26 Estimated Outcome:

The variance in the funding ratio is mainly due to reinsurance recoveries in the medical negligence and property classes, lower outstanding claims provisions that are largely driven by lower than expected inflation, and changes to actuarial assumptions in relation to medical negligence experience.

Lower general and administrative expense as a percentage of total annual premium revenue is mainly related to lower than anticipated expenses in supplies and services.

The variation in the average number of days taken to process payments is driven by court settlements, which allow a 28-day payment period. This timeline intersects with the Authority’s operational fortnightly pay schedule. The Authority ensures that all urgent payments are processed promptly and in accordance with claim settlement terms and court orders. No fines or penalties were incurred for settlement payments during the year.

Monitoring and Reporting

The Authority will satisfy the requirements of the Chief Minister’s Annual Reports Directions. The Authority’s Annual Report will, among other things, report against the requirements of this Statement of Intent.

The FMA authorises the Treasurer to obtain financial and other statements from the Authority for a stated period including monthly, quarterly and annual reporting.

Monthly Reporting

The Authority prepares monthly reports that are available to the Treasurer through CMTEDD by the eighth working day of each month. The reports will be presented in the prescribed form and required detail in respect of the previous calendar month. These reports include:

an Operating Statement;

a Balance Sheet; and

a Cash Flow Statement.

Quarterly Reporting

To enable consolidated whole of government reporting requirements to be met on a quarterly basis, the Authority will ensure the availability of the previous quarter’s Management discussion and Analysis to the Treasurer, through CMTEDD, by the tenth working day of each quarter. This will be presented in the prescribed form and detail, and show results to date, forecast results and related issues that may impact on the financial condition of the Authority.

Annual Reporting

As part of preparations for end of year reporting CMTEDD will advise the dates when the following documents are required by the ACT Audit Office and to CMTEDD. These reports include:

certified financial statements;

management discussion and analysis;

a full and accurate set of audited financial records for the preceding financial year in the form requested; and

consolidation packs relating to the annual financial statements, draft and final.


<a id="financial-arrangements"></a>

### Financial Arrangements

The Authority operates on a cost recovery basis by collecting premiums from directorates and statutory authorities to meet the cost of insurable claims and losses. The Authority’s operating costs are largely driven by provisioning for future and current claims expense.

The Authority’s 2025-26 estimated outcome is an operating surplus of $48.7 million. The result is a $38 million increase from the original budgeted operating result of $10.6 million in the 2025‑26 Budget.

The variance to the original budget is largely a timing issue with a number of contributing factors. These factors include lower than anticipated claims expenses that are largely driven by lower than expected inflation based on the 2025-26 mid-year actuarial review.

This variance is also impacted by the movement in the fair value of investments, mainly due to the recent changes in macroeconomic conditions leading to a slightly lower than targeted return on investment capital gains.

The Authority also performs the functions of the DIF for default claims under the ACT Private Workers’ Compensation Scheme and the NOD for claims against uninsured and unidentified vehicles under the ACT Motor Accident Injury Scheme. The Authority provides support services on a cost recovery basis to these schemes and recovers these costs from DIF and NOD on a quarterly basis.

Budgeted Financial Statements

Budgeted financial statements for the 2026-27 Budget year, as well as the three forward estimates years appear below. These general-purpose financial statements have been prepared in accordance with the ACT’s Model Financial Statements and include:

an Operating Statement;

a Balance Sheet;

a Statement of Changes in Equity; and

a Cash Flow Statement.

Financial Statements

Presentational changes have been made to streamline the financial statements, and the

2026-27 Budget column also reflects this change.  These changes will be consistent with future annual financial statements and ensure comparability of the annual financial statements with the budget estimates as required under section 27 of the Financial Management Act 1996.

<a id="notes-to-the-controlled-budget-statements-2"></a>

#### Notes to the Controlled Budget Statements

Significant variations are as follows:

Operating Statement

sale of goods and services from contracts with customers: the $9.4 million increase in the 2026‑27 Budget compared to the 2025-26 estimated outcome for annual insurance premiums is primarily driven by anticipated agency premiums for 2026-27 and a deterioration in claims experience across property, medical negligence and motor vehicle classes. This increase also reflects global market conditions, which are expected to drive higher reinsurance costs for the Territory, as well as actuarial valuations of ACTIA’s breakeven premium required to cover the Territory’s retained risk and associated costs.

investment revenue: the increase of $1.4 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is due to the budget estimate being based on targeted return on investment as per the Authority’s long term investment plan and investment strategy of making a targeted annual return on investment of CPI plus 2.5 per cent, and the portfolio mix to achieve the intended ratio for investment distribution and capital gains.

gain on investments:

the increase of $6.7 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is due to the fair value of investments having increased, leading to a higher than targeted return on investment capital gains; and

the decrease of $6.5 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is due to the budget estimate being based on a targeted return on investment as per the Authority’s long term investment plan, and investment strategy of making a targeted annual return on investment at long-term CPI plus 2.5 per cent, which is lower than the recent gains achieved.

other income: the increase of $32.7 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget and the subsequent decrease of $32 million in the 2026-27 Budget reflect an estimate for claims related reinsurance recovery receivables in 2025-26 related to the large fire event at the Hume Material Recovery Facility.

claims expenses: the increase of $13 million in the 2026-27 Budget from 2025‑26 estimated outcome is due to the movement in actuarial estimates for the Authority’s claims liabilities resulting from large claims for significant fire events at the Hume Material Recovery Facility, along with deteriorating claims experience in medical malpractice.

operating result: the increase of $38 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original 2025-26 Budget and a subsequent decrease of $41.3 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is explained below.

the spike in other income in the 2025-26 estimated outcome reflects an estimate for claims related reinsurance recovery receivables in 2025-26 related to the large fire event at the Hume Material Recovery Facility.

total expenses decreased by $6.8 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025‑26 Budget due to lower expenses in employee costs, administration, outward re‑insurance and claims expenses.

total expenses increased by $13.2 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025‑26 estimated outcome due to an increase in expectations of claims in outward re-insurance and general ACTIA claims expenses and liabilities returning to the longer-term claims experience pattern, based on estimations in the 2025-26 mid-year actuaries’ valuation review.

Balance Sheet

cash and cash equivalents: the increase of $33.4 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget and subsequent $39.5 million increase in the 2026-27 Budget is mainly due to the expectation of improved operating cash flow and the requirement to hold a larger than average cash balance in the bank to manage larger payments for claims experience, and the expectation of reinsurance recoveries returning to the longer term investments.

current receivables: the increase of $6 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome compared to the 2025-26 Budget is attributable primarily to higher fair value gains on investments, reflecting stronger-than-anticipated market performance amid prevailing economic conditions. This has resulted in returns exceeding targeted investment capital gains. From 2026-27, receivables are expected to normalise and return to a more stable base as market conditions moderate.

non-current receivables: the increase of $18.5 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due to reinsurance recoveries receivables for large claims in 2025‑26, and the $13.7 million decrease from the 2025-26 estimated outcome to the 2026‑27 budget is mainly due to an expectation of deteriorating claims experience.

current other provisions: the increase of $24 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget and subsequent $3.9 million increase in the 2026-27 Budget is due mainly to the 2025-26 mid-year actuarial valuation review.

non-current other provisions:

the decrease of $10 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due to lower claims expenses, largely driven by lower than expected inflation, claims experience, and assumption changes in the medical negligence class based on the 2025‑26 mid-year actuarial valuation review; and

the increase of $16.8 million from the 2025-26 estimated outcome and the 2026-27 Budget is mainly due to the anticipated cost of future liabilities, as a result of deteriorating claims experience and the 2025-26 mid-year actuarial valuation review.

Statement of Changes in Equity and Cash Flow Statement

Variations in these Statements are explained in the notes above including net effect of change in accounting policy AASB17.

<a id="canberra-institute-of-technology-statement-of-intent"></a>

## CANBERRA INSTITUTE OF TECHNOLOGY – STATEMENT OF INTENT

The Canberra Institute of Technology (CIT) is a Territory Authority established under the Canberra Institute of Technology Act 1987 (the Act).

This Statement of Intent for 2026-27 has been prepared in accordance with Section 61 of the Financial Management Act 1996 (the FMA).

The responsible Minister, Mr Michael Pettersson MLA, was consulted during the preparation of the Statement of Intent.

The Statement of Intent, which focuses on the 2026-27 Budget year, has been developed in the context of a four year forward planning horizon to be incorporated, as far as practicable, into the CIT strategic and business planning processes.

The CIT 2026-27 Statement of Intent has been agreed between:


|   |   |   |
|---|---|---|
|   |   |   |
| Chris Steel MLATreasurer    June 2026 |   | Michael Pettersson MLAMinister for Skills        May 2026 |


|   |

| Tom RogersChair, CIT Board     May 2026 |

<a id="canberra-institute-of-technology"></a>

## CANBERRA INSTITUTE OF TECHNOLOGY


<a id="purpose-3"></a>

### Purpose

The purpose of the Canberra Institute of Technology (CIT), relevant to the Statement of Intent, is to:

support the ACT Government’s Skilled to Succeed: skills and workforce agenda for the ACT by partnering with industry, customers and government to develop targeted training programs that are responsive to the needs of individuals in the community who have a desire or willingness to learn, fostering a future-focused skills sector.

perform the role of public provider of vocational education and training (VET) in the ACT in accordance with its functions under the Canberra Institute of Technology Act 1987 (the Act), with course offerings aligned to ACT Government priorities for a skilled workforce.

support the ACT to pursue economic growth and sustainability by offering up-to-date learning opportunities applying emerging technologies for students to acquire the relevant skills for current and future workforce requirements.

foster inclusive and culturally appropriate learning environments, ensuring that accessible education pathways are available to all in the community including students from diverse backgrounds, those with disabilities and underrepresented communities. CIT Yurauna is CIT's dedicated Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander centre for educational excellence, and it supports First Nations students across the CIT.


<a id="nature-and-scope-of-activities-2"></a>

### Nature and Scope of Activities


<a id="general-activities-2"></a>

#### General Activities

CIT is the ACT’s public provider of vocational education and training, the cornerstone of the local skills and training system and deeply embedded in the ACT community. Established in 1928, CIT has a rich history in supporting economic growth, community development and providing individuals with opportunities to meet their learning and career goals. CIT has campuses in Bruce, Fyshwick, Tuggeranong, Gungahlin and the new state-of-the-art campus at Woden.

CIT is dedicated to collaborating closely with the ACT Government to respond to the territory’s evolving skills requirements. Through ongoing dialogue and partnership and taking advantage of advancements in technology CIT is working towards course offerings and training programs that are aligned with both current workforce demands and emerging industry trends. This commitment enables CIT to proactively support the ACT Government’s strategic objectives, fostering a skilled and adaptable workforce equipped for the challenges and opportunities of a dynamic economy.

With CIT’s experience in delivering quality vocational education and training, with expertise in training in trades, IT, care and service industries CIT is also well positioned to harness the opportunities arising from the current national policy framework which places TAFE at the heart of the response to current and emerging skills needs.  CIT has been recognised Nationally with the Electric Vehicle TAFE Centre of Excellence and the Cyber Security TAFE Centre of Excellence.

CIT’s strategic vision is to be renowned for our inclusive and dynamic approach to teaching and learning that meets the needs of students, industry and the community. Strong partnerships and engagement with our people, students, industry, the ACT community and stakeholders are at the heart of our strategic delivery approach to meet current and future skills needs locally, nationally and globally.


<a id="risks"></a>

#### Risks

CIT recognises effective risk management is integral to quality management practices and is working to embed risk management processes to further enhance decision-making and resources utilisation.  CIT has an organisational-wide approach to risk management as an integral part of its overall governance framework and business and operational processes are underpinned by a risk management framework. The CIT Strategic Risk Register is used to identify and monitor key areas of risk which are regularly reviewed to ensure it continues to highlight relevant risks and CIT’s exposure.

Risks for CIT are considered through a combination of quality assurances assessments, spot checks, internal and external reviews, including the monitoring of implementation of recommendations from these reviews through the Executive Management Committee, Audit and Risk Committee and the CIT Board.  CIT employs mitigation strategies including staff training programs, and incident reporting mechanisms to manage identified risks.

Risk controls and treatments, and emerging risks, are monitored through the Executive Management Committee, CIT Audit and Risk Committee and CIT Board. CIT also has an overarching Fraud and Corruption Prevention Framework, which is to be reviewed in 2026.

CIT engages with compliance and regulatory bodies including ACT Government and Skills Canberra, Australian Skills Quality Authority (ASQA) and Australian Nursing & Midwifery Accreditation Council (ANMAC) to ensure reviews of CIT’s governance meet the relevant standards.


<a id="2026-27-priorities-and-next-three-financial-years-2"></a>

### 2026-27 Priorities and Next Three Financial Years

CIT’s 10-Year Strategic Plan 2025-2035: Skilling for the future, equipping you for life outlines four strategic objectives:

Our training meets the skills needs of today, tomorrow and beyond: responsive to skills needs through the scaling, adaptation and expansion of our course profile.

Our teaching and learning is leading edge and enhances student outcomes: designing and implementing inclusive teaching and learning environments and innovative in our teaching practice.

Our people are equipped to achieve our strategy: creating an environment where our people thrive, are motivated, and embrace leading roles in achieving our ambitions.

Our foundations support the delivery of our strategy and enable future growth: building places, spaces, systems and processes that are underpinned by, and enable our values and strategy delivery.

CIT has projected delivery under this strategy across three horizons, the first of which is the enabling phase, with a focus on embedding the strategy, enabling CIT’s workforce, and strengthening CIT’s foundations to support delivery.

Priorities through this phase include:

National Vocational Education and Training (VET) Reform: the national VET landscape is changing, initiatives under the National Skills Agreement (NSA) are maturing with Jurisdictional Action Plans now in place in response to the inaugural National Skills Plan. CIT will progress work on NSA funded programs including the Electric Vehicle Centre of Excellence, VET Workforce Blueprint and the Cyber Security Centre. CIT is also working with Skills Canberra on the National TAFE Network (noting dependencies with the Centre of Excellence model).

Profile Planning: As part of its commitment to strategic growth and excellence, CIT is transitioning to a more sophisticated approach to profile planning. This approach ensures that program offerings are closely aligned with both the ACT Government’s priorities and CIT’s own long-term strategic objectives. By adopting this targeted planning process, CIT will be able to better respond to evolving skills needs and deliver greater value to its students and stakeholders. Consequently, programs that do not directly support these strategic directions may be deprioritised, allowing CIT to focus resources on areas of greatest impact and future relevance, while maintaining sensitivity to the needs of its diverse community.

Growth and Diversification: to meet the charter set by the CIT Strategy 2025-2035, and respond to local and national skills needs, CIT must position itself for future growth. In 2024, the CIT Board endorsed CIT’s Future Growth Strategy. The strategy is designed to support achievement of CIT’s financial sustainability within the medium term, realisation of CIT’s bold ambitions for national leadership in skills training for tomorrow and beyond, and lay a sustainable foundation for the future of the Institute.

Learning Design & Development: CIT will articulate its vision for the future of teaching and learning, aligned to the CIT Strategy, and ensure capability to design and deliver programs that meet CIT’s future requirements.

Student Experience: to further enhance and support student centricity as a core Value, CIT will continue to build on the foundations of making the student experience our top priority. This includes the creation of a Student Reference Group to provide ongoing feedback and direction from the student body as well as the creation of a Student Experience Strategy and agreed approach to active Alumni engagement. Focus will also be placed on marketing and recruitment activities and process improvements that align with new technologies implemented through the Cloud Campus program.

Building Capability & Strengthening Culture:  CIT is committed to fostering a respectful, collaborative and accountable workplace culture where staff feel valued and supported to excel. Guided by the CIT Strategic Plan 2025-2035, CIT People Plan 2025-2028 and the national VET Blueprint, our focus is on creating an inclusive, high-performing environment that enables our people to deliver innovative, industry-responsive vocational education. Across these frameworks, we emphasise strengthening organisational culture through reconciliation, gender equity, safety, wellbeing and a shared commitment to excellence.

To build the capability needed for future workforce and sector transformation, CIT is prioritising targeted professional learning, strategic workforce attraction and retention, and enhanced workforce intelligence to inform evidence-based decisions. National investment in VET workforce strengthening supports CIT’s organisational realignment, digital and hybrid learning initiatives, and ongoing capability development. Collectively, these efforts ensure our people are equipped for emerging challenges, aligned to institutional and national priorities, and empowered to contribute to improved student outcomes and a more agile, future-ready organisation.

Academic Quality & Integrity: critical to CIT’s success is a continued focus on ensuring academic quality and integrity across the Institute. Governance, systems and processes will continue to be strengthened to ensure credibility through high quality products and delivery, aligned to the Revised Standards for Registered Training Organisations.

Digital Transformation: CIT is continuing its digital transformation journey, ensuring CIT is equipped with the capability to realise our digital transformation vision to improve the lives of our students through incredible teaching that takes full advantage of available technologies.

Campus Renewal & Management: CIT utilises capital funding through the asset renewal program for the continued uplift of campus facilities, ensuring they remain modern, fit for purpose, and able to support the evolving needs of students and staff in the delivery of training.

<a id="strategic-objectives-and-indicator"></a>

### Strategic Objectives and Indicator


<a id="strategic-objective"></a>

#### Strategic Objective

Student Outcomes

CIT’s objective is to provide training that meets student needs ensuring a job ready skill set for our future workers.

Strategic Indicator: Student Outcomes Survey

The key strategic indicator used by CIT to measure its success is students’ employment outcomes as measured through the annual National Centre for Vocational Education Research (NCVER) Student Outcomes Survey. By focusing on student employment outcomes and satisfaction with VET, the Student Outcomes Survey is an indicative measure of how well CIT serves individuals and the community.

Quality and effectiveness can be measured through student outcomes against national performance. When compared against national TAFE performance, CIT students consistently achieve high levels of employment. The 2025 performance of CIT graduates, against national performance for Australian TAFE graduates, is highlighted in Figure 1 below. As illustrated, CIT graduates exceed or are in line with national performance on all measures.

Figure 1: NCVER Performance of CIT Graduates Against Other Australian TAFE Graduates 2025 (%)

Notes and Explanation of Material Variances:

This is a measure of the total student enrolments in each individual unit of competency for the reporting period, excluding any enrolments relating to students who withdrew without attendance.  The 2025-26 Estimated Outcome are above the 2025-26 Targets due to enrolments in programs previously delivered by CIT Solutions that have transferred to CIT.

2025 Subject enrolments were below the 2025 target due to a strong local employment market reducing the immediate demand for training.

This is a measure of the proportion of subject enrolments issued with a grade indicating successful completion of the unit of competency during the reporting period, expressed as a percentage of total subject enrolments during the same period.

This is a measure of the proportion of current CIT students participating in the VET Student Experience Survey (VETSES) who indicated that they were satisfied with the training at CIT.

The employer satisfaction rate measures the proportion of employers with apprentices or trainees at CIT that expressed their satisfaction with the training delivered. The online survey was sent to all current employers of apprentices enrolled at CIT in the respective period and uses questions set by the Australian Skills Quality Authority (ASQA). This outcome is consistent with previous years reflecting the positive views of employers and the experiences as employees of CIT apprentices.

<a id="monitoring-and-reporting"></a>

### Monitoring and Reporting

CIT complies with the requirements of the Annual Reports Directions.  The CIT Annual Report will, among other things, report against the requirements of this Statement of Intent.

The FMA authorises the Treasurer to obtain financial and other statements from the CIT for a stated period including annual, quarterly and monthly reporting.


<a id="quarterly-reporting"></a>

#### Quarterly Reporting

To enable consolidated whole of government reporting requirements to be met on a quarterly basis, the CIT will ensure the availability to the Treasurer, through CMTEDD (by the eighth working day of each quarter, unless otherwise indicated), information, in the prescribed form and detail, in respect of the previous quarter:

Operating Statement

Balance Sheet

Statement of Changes in Equity

Cash Flow Statement

Operating Statement material variance explanations against seasonal budget

Status Report to supplement performance reporting to the Assembly and provide stakeholders with a summary on progress against budget highlights, significant initiatives and major projects

Financial Management Analysis of results to date, forecast results and related issues that may impact on the financial condition of the CIT


<a id="annual-reporting"></a>

#### Annual Reporting

In accordance with CIT’s financial reporting requirements, CIT will provide the Auditor General’s Office the following documents in accordance with calendar year reporting timeframes:

Certified financial statements

Management discussion and analysis

Statement of Performance

Consolidation packs relating to CIT’s financial year results will be provided to CMTEDD for consolidation in the ACT Government financial statements.


<a id="financial-arrangements-2"></a>

### Financial Arrangements

Budgeted financial statements for the 2026-27 Budget year, as well as forward estimates for the three financial years appear below.  These general-purpose financial statements have been prepared in accordance with the ACT’s Model Financial Statements and include:

Operating Statement

Balance Sheet

Statement of Changes in Equity

Cash Flow Statement

Notes to the Financial Statements as appropriate.

<a id="notes-to-the-calendar-year-financial-statements"></a>

#### Notes to the Calendar Year Financial Statements

Significant variations are as follows:

Operating Statement

There are no significant variations in budget and actual outcomes in the operating statement.

Balance Sheet

property, plant and equipment:

the decrease of $44.045 million in the 2025 actual from the 2025 Budget is due to CIT Yurauna building expected to be transferred from Infrastructure Canberra in 2026 compared to the budget assumption of 2025.

the increase of $18.210 million in the 2025 actual to the 2026 Budget is due to the transfer of CIT Yurauna from Infrastructure Canberra in 2026 partially offset by depreciation expense to be recognised on existing assets.

assets held for distribution to owners: the increase of the 2025 actual from the 2025 and 2026 Budget of $44.560 million is primarily due to assumed timing of transfer of Reid Campus from CIT to Infrastructure Canberra to which the associated provision for remediation works to be completed at the site under the University of New South Wales Masterplan.

other provisions: the increase of the 2025 actual from the 2025 and 2026 Budget of $42.092 million and $40.951 million respectively is due to assumed timing of transfer of Reid Campus from CIT to Infrastructure Canberra to which the associated provision for remediation works to be completed at the site under the University of New South Wales Masterplan.

Statement of Changes in Equity

Variations in the statement of changes in equity are explained in the notes above.

Cash Flow Statement

There are no significant variations in budget and actual outcomes in the cash flow statement.

<a id="city-renewal-authority-statement-of-intent"></a>

## CITY RENEWAL AUTHORITY – STATEMENT OF INTENT

The City Renewal Authority (City Renewal) is a Territory Authority established under the City Renewal Authority and Suburban Land Agency Act 2017.

City Renewal was established to lead the coordination and implementation of world-class urban renewal within the declared City Renewal Precinct. The establishment of City Renewal ensures that as Canberra grows, its city centre and immediate surrounds will further evolve into a thriving precinct with well-designed buildings and popular public spaces that improve quality of life, prosperity and sustainability.

This Statement of Intent has been prepared in accordance with section 61 of the Financial Management Act 1996.

The responsible Minister, Chief Minister Andrew Barr MLA, was consulted during the preparation of the Statement. The Statement, which focuses on the 2026-27 Budget year, has been developed in the context of a four year forward planning horizon to be incorporated, as far as practicable, into City Renewal’s strategic and business planning processes.

The City Renewal Authority 2026-27 Statement of Intent has been agreed between:


|   |   |   |   |
|---|---|---|---|
|   |   |   |   |
| Ms Marcia Doheny Chair City Renewal Authority21 May 2026 |   | Mr Andrew Barr MLAChief Minister     June 2026 | Mr Chris Steel MLATreasurer     June 2026 |
|   |   |   |   |

This page deliberately left blank


<a id="city-renewal-authority"></a>

## CITY RENEWAL AUTHORITY


<a id="purpose-4"></a>

### Purpose

City Renewal was established under the City Renewal Authority and Suburban Land Agency Act 2017 (the CRASLA Act) in 2017 to undertake and facilitate urban renewal projects in declared urban renewal precincts.

Working with the community, City Renewal recognises that the places that define our city are for its citizens to live and work in, enjoy and support a quality-of-life Canberrans expect and deserve. These key factors are reflected in the ACT Government’s Wellbeing Framework, to which City Renewal’s priorities and objectives are aligned.

The objects of City Renewal are set out in section 8 of the CRASLA Act and include:

the encouragement and promotion of a vibrant city through the delivery of design-led, people -focused urban renewal

the encouragement and promotion of social and environmental sustainability

operational effectiveness, delivering value for money using sound risk practices.

City Renewal’s functions are set out in section 9 of the CRASLA Act and include:

carrying out urban renewal

buying and selling leases of land on behalf of the Territory

making arrangements for the public service or another entity to carry out development or works

supporting public and private sector investment and participation in urban renewal

managing orderly urban renewal, including holding, managing and selling land and other property

supporting cooperation between City Renewal, the community and relevant entities

supporting high quality design, planning and delivery of sustainable urban renewal

meeting housing targets determined under section 65 of the CRASLA Act that relate to affordable, community and public housing

supporting statutory greenhouse gas emissions targets and delivering environmentally sustainable development

following and supporting whole-of-government strategies

any other function given to City Renewal under the CRASLA Act or another Territory law.

City Renewal must exercise its functions in a way that is consistent with its Statement of Operational Intent (as required by section 9 of the CRASLA Act).


<a id="nature-and-scope-of-activities-3"></a>

### Nature and Scope of Activities

City Renewal is charged with shaping the growth of central Canberra, envisioning the City Centre as a captivating gathering place where creativity, work, life, and leisure come together.

Its mission is to transform Canberra’s City Centre into a contemporary and magnetic destination by creating culturally rich, sustainable, and thoughtfully-designed places, facilitating new investment, celebrating unique characteristics, attracting people to local businesses, engaging with the community, and activating welcoming, lively spaces.

City Renewal delivers design-led and people-focused urban renewal with a focus on social and environmental sustainability, working within the City Renewal Precinct across Dickson, Braddon, the City Centre, Haig Park, and Acton Waterfront.

With a focus on Caring for Country, City Renewal works with the community, business sector and government to create places within the Precinct that are of exemplary design quality, excite interest and participation, stimulate new investment into the Precinct, and reflect Ngunnawal cultural heritage through engagement with their Elders.

City Renewal activates the Precinct with events, interventions and attractions that bring people into the city and enliven the experience for residents, workers and visitors.

City Renewal aims to deliver the following outcomes within the City Renewal Precinct:

the curation of high-quality places and precinct development that takes a people-focused and design-led approach

the facilitation of new and diverse investment into the Precinct

the promotion and application of robust and innovative social and environmental sustainability principles and programs that underpin precinct-wide renewal

being a strong, strategic, influential and capable organisation

demonstrating exemplary accountability and transparency in governance and compliance.


<a id="2026-27-priorities-2"></a>

### 2026-27 Priorities

Consistent with the Government’s Statement of Expectations, City Renewal will continue implementation of the City Precinct Renewal Program, including the following priorities for 2026-27 and across the forward years.

Delivery across the program includes a mix of construction activity and earlier-stage work such as planning, design, technical investigations, approvals, market engagement and due diligence. Accordingly, where activity is deferred, rephased or progressed through non-construction stages, this refers to the timing and sequencing of delivery rather than a change in the underlying program objective. These activities remain part of program delivery because they inform future investment decisions, maintain readiness for later stages and support the coordinated development of the Precinct.

City Spine program – the City Spine is the major circulation corridor within the City Centre that connects City East (including the future University of New South Wales (UNSW) Canberra City campus) and City West (including the Australian National University campus). The program of public realm upgrades is being implemented in stages:

stage 1 (City Walk: Garema Place to Ainslie Place) was completed in 2020

construction of stage 2 (Garema Place- Stage 1) will be completed in 2025-26

preliminary designs for stages 3 (City Walk: Ainslie Place to Allara Street) and 4 (Alinga Street) were completed in 2025-26.

paving and lighting upgrades to improve safety in the City Centre will be progressively completed until 2027

further public realm upgrades along City Walk, from East Row to Garema Place, will occur by 2028

City streetscape program – the City Streetscape program consists of public realm upgrades across the City Centre to improve connectivity. City Renewal is progressing masterplanning for Alinga Street to better connect the City Centre with businesses and residences in City West and the Australian National University.

Acton Waterfront program – City Renewal is charged with developing a new mixed-use precinct at West Basin, which will improve connectivity with the city centre and Lake Burley Griffin.  The Acton Waterfront Boardwalk, including land reclamation and Henry Rolland Park are complete. Over the next four years, City Renewal will progress planning and development works for the new Acton Waterfront Neighbourhood. Construction commenced on the new waterfront park, known as Ngamawari in April 2026 and is forecast for completion in 2028.

Dickson program – City Renewal is undertaking a program of public realm improvements across the Dickson Group Centre, including the already completed Woolley Street upgrade. The next stages include:

construction of public realm upgrades at the Dickson Shops, including upgrades to the underlying stormwater network, due to be completed by December 2027.

master planning for Section 72 will commence, including a community needs analysis

City Hill – City Renewal is progressing a concept masterplan for City Hill park to guide its future renewal as a significant urban park within the heart of the city. Informed by technical and heritage studies and a comprehensive community and stakeholder engagement program, including a People’s Panel, the masterplan will articulate a clear vision for City Hill and establish the design objectives, principles, options and staging framework to support future investment, design and construction.

Canberra Civic and Cultural District – City Renewal is finalising master planning for the precinct and continuing work on technical investigations and market engagement for Block 23, Section 19 City to prepare the site for release as a mixed-use cultural precinct under the ACT Government’s land release program in 2027-28.

UNSW Canberra City campus – City Renewal will continue to oversee the Precinct Deed between the Territory and UNSW. In this capacity, City Renewal will support the implementation of the approved Campus Master Plan for a new campus in Reid.

Precinct activation – City Renewal will continue its comprehensive annual program of place curation, activation, and promotion of the public spaces across the Precinct, which includes popular events such as Winter in the City and La Fiesta.

Place management – City Renewal will continue to deliver an elevated program of place management and maintenance of public spaces across the Precinct.

Future land sales – City Renewal will continue feasibility and due diligence for the future sale of Territory land within the Precinct, in accordance with the ACT Government’s Housing Supply and Land Release Program, and identify new land development opportunities, including in the City South-East district.

Development Application referrals – City Renewal will continue to provide informed advice to the City and Environment Directorate for all referred development applications within the Precinct to support high quality design, sustainability, and community wellbeing outcomes.


<a id="risk-management-approach"></a>

#### Risk management approach

City Renewal is committed to ensuring that all business and operational processes are underpinned by an effective risk management process.

Its risk management framework provides the foundation and organisational arrangements for how risk is managed across City Renewal. This framework assists in achieving objectives and ensures risk management processes are embedded consistently. It integrates the process for managing risk into City Renewal’s overall governance, strategy and planning, management, reporting processes, policies, values and culture.

Strategic risks and responses are outlined within the framework and take account of internal and external factors that may affect its ability to effectively deliver the City Precinct Renewal Program.

City Renewal ensures a sound and proactive culture of risk management exists in the organisation and that processes are implemented to support continuous improvement and review, including:

regular assessment of risk management processes to identify opportunities for improvement

regular reviews of models, frameworks, and standards used in other organisations to ensure that its framework continues to reflect contemporary best practice

ongoing training and development for relevant teams and staff to ensure they are equipped with a sound knowledge and skills base

inclusion of, and measurement against, performance measures relating to City Renewal’s risk management and other key governance processes

conducting a risk-based internal audit program.

City Renewal maintains risk registers for the following categories:

Enterprise (strategic and major operational risks)

Project and program

Workplace Health and Safety (WHS)

Fraud and corruption risks

City Renewal’s risk management framework is approved by its Board and includes risk appetite statements. These signal the Board’s appetite for various types of risks and where it may be prepared to accept risk or not. This guides decision makers and contributes to achieving our strategic objectives. There are four levels of risk appetite:

Avoid – little to no appetite (Avoidance of risk and uncertainty is key to achieving objectives)

Averse – small appetite (Prefer safe options with little risk of adverse exposure)

Accept – medium appetite (Consider all options and choose most likely for successful delivery with reasonable degree of protection)

Receptive – large appetite (Will engage with risks and opportunities when the potential benefit is great).

City Renewal manages risks in its operations related to achievement of its objectives, designing and developing quality precincts, public engagement and participation, resource management and compliance with complex requirements.

Gross Profit Margin = (lease sales – cost of goods sold) / lease sales

The profit margins of City Renewal are used for internal purposes to provide comparatives across projects and time periods. These margins are not comparable with private industry and should be considered in context with the following:

In general, the Government enters the land development process at an earlier stage than private developers as it has responsibility for planning activities that can impact on zoning and lease conditions on the land. The uplift in the value of land attributable to these activities is recognised by City Renewal and forms part of its return to Government.

The Government invests in significant infrastructure within and around the City Renewal Precinct, but these costs are often incurred by other government agencies and therefore do not impact on City Renewal’s profit margins.

City Renewal relies on independent market valuations to inform land sales prices.

Excludes capital injections not related to infrastructure works.
