<a id="explanation-of-table-6-2025-26-performance-measure-outcomes"></a>

##### Explanation of Table 6 2025-26 performance measure outcomes

City Spine program

City Renewal is upgrading Garema Place to create a modern, exciting dining and retail precinct in the City Centre, featuring:

increased green space and tree cover

less clutter and improved pedestrian flow

better accessibility, lighting and other safety features

more outdoor dining opportunities, supported by a new Garema Place Outdoor Dining Guideline.

Construction of the main area of Garema Place was completed in December 2025. Work on the City Walk section began in January 2026. The project was delivered in stages to reduce disruption to surrounding businesses. Completion is scheduled for 2026.

Acton Waterfront program

City Renewal is progressing the Acton Waterfront neighbourhood, a place-led initiative to create a connected, mixed-use waterfront precinct linking the City Centre to Lake Burley Griffin. The Urban Design Framework sets out the design principles and considerations to guide future development outcomes. The Urban Design Framework is scheduled for submission to the National Capital Authority Board in late May to early June 2026.

Dickson program

City Renewal is upgrading the public realm surrounding the Dickson shops to create a sustainable and attractive public environment that encourages people to visit and stay longer, driving social and economic activity in the process. Some of the key features of the upgrade include:

improved paving and landscaping

enhanced lighting and other safety features

improved seating and signage

improved active travel connections

upgrades to the underlying stormwater network to address flooding issues in the courtyards.

CED provided Design Accepted in July 2026.

A managing contractor was procured, with the contract executed in October 2025. Preliminary works and investigations began in November 2025. Main construction commenced in January 2026. Completion is scheduled for 2027.

Land Development

City Renewal released Section 116, City (the City Hill surface carpark opposite 1 Constitution Avenue) to market via a two-stage process in April 2026, as part of the Territory’s 2025-26 Housing Supply and Land Release Program.

The primarily residential mix-use development will deliver a minimum of 742 new dwellings, including 55 community and 55 affordable dwellings.

Precinct activation

City Renewal successfully delivered the following events in 2025-26. These activations contributed to increased footfall in the city, improving economic and wellbeing outcomes:

Winter in the City (July 2025), a 15 day program of free entertainment including daily circus, theatre and music shows and nightly fire performances. The program delivered 76 individual performances and attracted more than 41,000 people with an estimated economic impact of $4.4 million.

La Fiesta (November 2025), which attracted over 7,000 attendees to City West for the annual Latin America Festival. The one-day event was support by numerous Latin America Embassies and featured eight hours of entertainment from more than 75 performers.

Christmas in the City 2025 (November/December 2025), which included the Civic Christmas tree and decorations across City Walk.

Dickson Lunar New Year celebrations marking the year of the Horse (February 2026), this annual event attracted 13,500 people and featured 12 hours of free programming from more than 250 performers across two stages and included stalls from 33 Canberra businesses.

Wonderful World (April 2025), a new family festival that featured a free six-day program of performances on the Glebe Park stage and a city-wide program of installations, entertainment and food experiences including 10 city businesses delivering tailored programming in their venues.

Precinct marketing

City Renewal contracted and successfully completed the following destination marketing campaigns in 2025-26:

Winter in the City 2025 campaign (June/July 2025)

Floriade 2025 Walking Trail (September/October 2025)

La Fiesta campaign (October/November 2025)

Christmas in the City 2025 campaign (November/December 2025)

Dickson Christmas campaign (December 2025)

Lunar New Year campaign (January/February 2026)

Business Grants to support the 2026 National Multicultural Festival (February 2026)

Wonderful World campaign (March 2026)

Winter in the City 2026 campaign (June/July 2026).

City Renewal also promoted many Precinct activations in 2025-26 via paid, earned and owned channels, including:

Plant Giveaways

Paws Parties in Haig Park

Placemaking Grant recipient events

Summernats Braddon Fringe Festival

Mother’s day in the City

Haig Park Village Markets.

Place management

City Renewal’s place management team continues to provide elevated maintenance with over 10,000 hours of cleaning, graffiti removal, planting and public space maintenance carried out each year. This includes planting over 10,000 annuals and bulbs across the Precinct to coincide with Floriade.

Communications and engagement

City Renewal undertook community engagement processes via YourSay for the following renewal projects in 2025-26:

City Hill park

Acton Waterfront neighbourhood.

City Renewal’s social media audience reach exceeded the 4.5 million target, with an estimated reach of more than 5 million.

City Centre Marketing and Improvements Levy (CCMIL)

City Renewal’s CCMIL Advisory Group meets every quarter to support ongoing planning to expend the levy. The group comprises City Renewal staff and representatives, such as business owners from the CCMIL area.

City Renewal expended over 90% of the levy in 2025-26. This contributed to delivery of City Renewal’s place management and activation program in the levy area including activity focused on areas impacted by capital works, such as targeted graffiti cleaning, activations, events and marketing campaigns.

Staff performance and capability

City Renewal exceeded all its staff performance and capability targets in 2025-26, which covers essential training and performance planning.

Work health and safety (WHS)

City Renewal met its WHS targets in 2025-26.


<a id="2025-26-renewal-activities"></a>

##### 2025-26 renewal activities

In addition to the accomplishments detailed above, in 2025-26 City Renewal’s urban renewal activities also included:

City Spine Program

City Renewal finalised and published the Dickson Streetscapes Masterplan, and the Better City Street - Cooyong Street Masterplan.

Masterplanning for the Better City Streets project at Alinga Street is progressing, as is the Better City Streets project at City Walk (Ainslie to Allara Streets).

Sydney and Melbourne Buildings

City Renewal worked with the Sydney and Melbourne Buildings owners to facilitate the repainting of the buildings’ facades and ensure they have a consistent appearance that reflects their heritage. The facades have been repainted in a colour scheme that is in accordance with the buildings’ Conservation Management Plan.

Canberra Civic and Cultural District

City Renewal has created a Canberra Civic and Cultural District Development Master Plan, in collaboration with key project stakeholders (such as Infrastructure Canberra, Canberra Museum and Gallery, and Libraries ACT), to guide the redevelopment and revitalisation of the area around Civic Square and the Canberra Theatre Centre to foster a thriving arts and cultural scene.

Following updates for the final lyric theatre designs, the Development Master Plan was finalised in 2025. Final stakeholder engagement will take place in mid-2026.

UNSW Canberra City Campus

City Renewal is the lead agency administering the Precinct Deed between the Territory and the University of New South Wales (UNSW) for delivery of the UNSW Canberra City campus in Reid. Stage 1 of the campus has secured all required Territory and National Capital Authority approvals, with Works Approval granted in August 2025 and the Development Deed and Holding Lease now in effect. Remediation works commenced in late 2025, with Stage 1 main construction underway and scheduled for completion by early 2028.

Land development

Land sales contribute to renewal and economic development of the Precinct. In addition to the release of Section 116, City, in 2025-26:

City Renewal continued detailed planning, market engagement and due diligence for the release of Block 23 Section 19, City (the surface car park opposite the Sydney Building), including completion of precinct planning, procurement of commercial and probity advisers, commencement of public consultation, and delivery of a market Registration of Interest process in April-May 2026, to support land release and sales processes programmed for 2027–28 as part of the Canberra Civic and Cultural District.

<a id="monitoring-and-reporting-2"></a>

### Monitoring and Reporting

City Renewal will meet its reporting requirements as described below.


<a id="monthly-reporting"></a>

#### Monthly Reporting

To meet whole of government reporting requirements on a monthly basis, City Renewal will ensure the timely availability of the financial statements to the Treasurer through the Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate (CMTEDD). The statements will be in the prescribed form and required detail, for the previous calendar month. These will be provided by the due dates set by Treasury. The statements include:

Statement of Comprehensive Income

Balance Sheet and

Cash Flow Statement.


<a id="annual-reporting-2"></a>

#### Annual Reporting

To comply with the Financial Management Act 1996 and to enable whole of government reporting requirements to be met, City Renewal will provide the following information to the Auditor-General and CMTEDD by the dates set by Treasury:

Audited Financial Statements

Statement of Performance

Management Discussion and Analysis and

Consolidation packs relating to the annual financial statements, draft and final.


<a id="ministerial-and-directorate-reporting"></a>

#### Ministerial and Directorate Reporting

City Renewal will provide timely, accurate and coordinated advice to Government, including the Minister, on significant issues in its core area of business, including (but not limited to):

matters for which the Government is likely to be accountable in the Legislative Assembly

important operational or budgetary issues, including management of its responsibilities under the Work Health and Safety Act 2011

City Renewal’s achievement of set housing targets

contributing to a report that details any land acquisition by City Renewal as required by the Government Agencies (Land Acquisition Reporting) Act 2018

matters related to its internal governance, including compliance with the Government’s framework for management of risk and

decisions made by City Renewal’s Board.


<a id="financial-arrangements-3"></a>

### Financial Arrangements

City Renewal’s financial arrangements support the delivery of its statutory functions and the ongoing implementation of the City Precinct Renewal Program. The 2026-27 Budget and forward estimates reflect the timing and profile of investment required across the program, including land release, public realm works, precinct planning and major project commitments. In this context, the financial statements should be read alongside City Renewal’s priorities and delivery program, as year-to-year movements in funding, revenue and expenditure reflect the sequencing of activity across the Precinct.

City Renewal receives operational funding for corporate costs and capital funding to deliver projects under the 30-year City Precinct Renewal Program. City Renewal self-funds its land release program and returns 100 per cent of net profit after tax to the Territory as dividends. The Budget reflects an amended dividend policy, effective from 2026-27, under which 100 per cent of net profit after tax is returned quarterly in arrears in the following year to smooth cash flows.

City Renewal’s land release activities are characterised by a low volume, high value profile, with significant investment and expenditure often required several years in advance of revenue realisation. This results in a timing mismatch between cash outflows and receipts and has necessitated a government equity injection to support working capital and replenish cash reserves pending the realisation of development returns.

In addition, the UNSW Canberra City campus project is generating land disposal costs, which are impacting operating results across the forward estimates.

The UNSW Canberra City campus project also results in equity funded liabilities, which affect liquidity metrics across the forward estimates.

Budgeted financial statements for the 2025-26 Budget year, as well as forward estimates for the three financial years appear below. These general-purpose financial statements have been prepared in accordance with the Territory’s Model Financial Statements and include:

Statement of Comprehensive Income

Balance Sheet

Statement of Changes in Equity and

Cash Flow Statement.

<a id="notes-to-the-controlled-budget-statements-4"></a>

#### Notes to the Controlled Budget Statements

Significant variations are as follows:


<a id="operating-statement-2"></a>

##### Operating Statement

sale of goods and services from contracts with customers: the decrease of $45.766 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget, and the corresponding increase of $45.766 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome, predominantly reflects the unscheduled settlement of a prepaid land sale and contracts exchanged that are yet to settle, due to outstanding contract conditions precedent.


<a id="balance-sheet-3"></a>

##### Balance Sheet

cash and equivalents:

the decrease of $24.521 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025‑26 Budget predominantly reflects lower receipts from land sales, an unbudgeted land premium payment, and the deferral of the 2025-26 interim dividend instalment to October 2026.

the increase of $38.926 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome predominantly reflects changes to the timing of cash payments for dividend distributions, following the transition to quarterly payments in arrears, consistent with proposed dividend policy amendments.

property, plant and equipment: the increase of $37.304 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome predominantly reflects capital works in progress for construction of Acton Ngamawari Park and various projects under the City Precinct Renewal Program.

other liabilities: the increase of $30.823 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome reflects the dividend payable to the Territory, consistent with revised payment terms under proposed dividend policy amendments.

financial liabilities: the decrease of $17.975 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget reflects the unwinding of a financing liability associated with a prepaid land sale, where settlement occurred earlier than expected due to contract conditions precedent being satisfied ahead of schedule.


<a id="cash-flow-statement"></a>

##### Cash Flow Statement

Notes are not provided where significant variances are explained in the notes above.

capital injections: the increase of $47.452 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025‑26 estimated outcome reflects City Renewals capital works infrastructure program, remediation payments to University of New South Wales and appropriation for land acquisition from Suburban Land Agency.

payment of dividend: the decrease of $34.602 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Budget, reflects dividends payable to the Territory based on the operating surplus, in conjunction with the deferral of the 2025-26 interim dividend instalment to October 2026 and amendment to City Renewal’s dividend payments terms to quarterly in arrears.

<a id="cultural-facilities-corporation-statement-of-intent"></a>

## CULTURAL FACILITIES CORPORATION – STATEMENT OF INTENT

The Cultural Facilities Corporation is a Territory Authority established under the Cultural Facilities Corporation Act 1997.

This Statement of Intent for 2026-27 has been prepared in accordance with Section 61 of the Financial Management Act 1996.

The responsible Minister, Mr Michael Pettersson MLA, was consulted during the preparation of the Statement of Intent.

The Statement of Intent, which focuses on the 2026-27 Budget year, has been developed in the context of a four year forward planning horizon to be incorporated, as far as practicable, into the Cultural Facilities Corporation’s strategic and business planning processes.

The Cultural Facilities Corporation’s 2026-27 Statement of Intent has been agreed between:

Prof. Mathew Trinca

Chairperson

Cultural Facilities Corporation

Michael Pettersson MLA

Minister for Business, Arts and Creative Industries

Chris Steel MLA

Treasurer

This page deliberately left blank


<a id="cultural-facilities-corporation"></a>

## CULTURAL FACILITIES CORPORATION


<a id="purpose-5"></a>

### Purpose

The Cultural Facilities Corporation (CFC) supports the ACT Government’s arts, culture, heritage and creative industries objectives through cultural leadership, creative development, stewardship of cultural assets, and delivery of inclusive cultural experiences for the Canberra community and visitors.

The CFC’s Strategic Priorities 2026-2030 position the organisation as a trusted cultural steward and a catalyst for creativity, inclusion, wellbeing and sustainability. Guided by respectful engagement with Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples and cultures, the CFC creates transformative cultural experiences through live performance, exhibitions, collections, heritage places and public programs.

The CFC’s strategic direction is organised around four pillars:

Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Peoples: Leadership, Culture and Cultural Integrity

Connection: Building Belonging Through Creativity

Growth: Transforming Canberra’s Cultural Landscape

Organisational Capability: Strengthening Foundations for the Future.


<a id="nature-and-scope-of-activities-4"></a>

### Nature and Scope of Activities

The CFC is a statutory corporation of the ACT Government and the ACT’s largest cultural organisation, managing a unique mix of performing arts, visual arts, social history and heritage venues across multiple sites. These places are:

the Canberra Theatre Centre

the Canberra Museum and Gallery, including the Nolan Collection

Lanyon Homestead

Calthorpes House

Mugga Mugga, and

Albert Hall

The CFC contributes to Canberra’s creative ecology while engaging in exchange with artists, organisations and audiences across Australia and beyond.

The Canberra Theatre Centre is the ACT’s leading performing arts venue, presenting a diverse program of theatre, dance, music and contemporary performance while supporting artistic ambition across a broad range of disciplines. Through partnerships with national companies, local artists, commercial producers and community organisations, the Canberra Theatre Centre plays a central role in the cultural life of Canberra and the broader region.

The delivery of a new 2,000-seat Lyric Theatre represents a transformational investment in Canberra’s cultural infrastructure and future creative capacity. Building on the Canberra Theatre Centre’s strong track record, the Lyric Theatre will significantly expand its ability to attract and present large-scale productions and major cultural events, positioning Canberra as a nationally recognised centre for performing arts and cultural leadership.

The Galleries, Museums and Heritage division of the CFC oversees creative, curatorial and public engagement work across Canberra Museum and Gallery including the Nolan Collection and the CFC’s heritage places, with responsibilities spanning collections management, exhibitions, social history, heritage interpretation and learning programs.

The Canberra Museum and Gallery is home to the ACT’s visual arts and social history collections, including the nationally significant Nolan Collection. Through exhibitions, programs and events, Canberra Museum and Gallery provides a platform for artists and communities, sparks dialogue and imagination, and shares the stories of Canberra and the region. The CFC continues to strengthen Canberra Museum and Gallery’s role as a cultural anchor within Canberra’s evolving Civic and Cultural District.

The CFC also manages a number of heritage-listed places that connect people with the social, cultural and architectural history of the ACT. Lanyon Homestead reflects both Aboriginal and European histories associated with the Murrumbidgee River corridor. Mugga Mugga preserves the domestic and working life of one of Canberra’s early rural communities, while Calthorpes House provides a rare and intact insight into domestic life in early Canberra.

Albert Hall and the CFC’s heritage places operate as important civic and cultural gathering spaces that support community connection, cultural participation and place-based storytelling. Through exhibitions, events, performances and public programs, these places contribute to Canberra’s cultural identity and sense of belonging.


<a id="risks-2"></a>

#### Risks

The CFC operates within an integrated strategic risk and assurance framework aligned with ACT Government governance requirements. Risk management is embedded across the organisation’s planning, operations and decision-making processes, supporting delivery of the CFC’s Strategic Priorities 2026-2030 while maintaining public trust, cultural integrity and organisational sustainability.

The CFC operates in a complex environment involving management of significant public assets, major infrastructure investment, evolving community expectations and large-scale public engagement activities. Strategic risks are considered as part of organisational planning and oversight and are regularly reviewed by the Board, Executive and Audit and Risk Committee.

The CFC recognises that innovation, cultural leadership and creative ambition require measured risk-taking. The organisation adopts a balanced risk appetite characterised by:

low tolerance for risks impacting cultural integrity, safety, governance or statutory compliance;

moderate tolerance for innovation, commercial activity and partnership-based growth aligned to public value; and

active management of financial, operational and workforce risks associated with infrastructure expansion and organisational change.

The CFC maintains a particularly strong focus on:

cultural integrity and respectful engagement with Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples and cultures;

operational readiness associated with the delivery of the Lyric Theatre;

workforce capability, wellbeing and safety;

financial sustainability and revenue diversification;

stewardship of collections, heritage places and public assets; and

maintaining community trust through transparent governance and effective stakeholder engagement.

The organisation has effectively zero tolerance, as far as is reasonably practicable, for risks that could result in serious harm to staff, visitors, artists, contractors or the public, or cause significant damage to collections, heritage assets or facilities.

Mitigation strategies are embedded within operational and strategic initiatives, including workforce capability development, systems modernisation, governance oversight, safety management, and strengthened partnership and engagement frameworks. Risk management is supported through ongoing monitoring, assurance activities, audit processes, and performance reporting.


<a id="2026-27-priorities-and-next-three-financial-years-3"></a>

### 2026-27 Priorities and Next Three Financial Years

Over the period 2026-27 to 2029-30, the CFC will continue to position itself as a leading cultural organisation and a key contributor to Canberra’s identity. This period represents a significant phase of organisational transformation, growth and renewal, driven by major cultural infrastructure investment, evolving community expectations and expanded opportunities for cultural participation and engagement.

A priority for the CFC will be the successful opening and operation of the new 2,000-seat Lyric Theatre and expanded Canberra Theatre Centre. This transformational project will significantly increase Canberra’s capacity to host major productions, attract new audiences and support artistic development, while strengthening the ACT’s visitor economy and cultural profile.

The CFC will further strengthen the role of Canberra Museum and Gallery as a cultural anchor within the Civic and Cultural District, while continuing to steward and activate the organisation’s heritage places as important civic and cultural gathering spaces.

In preparation for this growth, the CFC will continue to build organisational capability, workforce capacity and operational readiness to support a larger and more complex operating environment. This includes strengthening governance, modernising systems and processes, enhancing workforce capability, and ensuring safe, sustainable and efficient operational delivery.

The CFC will continue to grow and diversify audiences through inclusive, high-quality programming and audience development initiatives across all sites. A strong focus will remain on improving access and participation, supporting local artists and creative practitioners, and delivering experiences that foster connection, belonging and wellbeing.

The organisation will continue to embed respectful engagement with Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples and cultures across programming, partnerships, workforce capability and governance practices. This includes supporting Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander-led storytelling, cultural leadership and authentic collaboration.

The organisation will also continue to strengthen long-term financial sustainability through diversification of revenue streams, including philanthropy, partnerships, commercial activation, memberships, ticketing and hospitality opportunities. These activities will support the CFC’s cultural ambitions while ensuring the organisation remains resilient, community-focused and financially sustainable.

The CFC’s strategic priorities are supported by integrated planning across programming, audience development, workforce capability, business systems, philanthropy, governance and risk management frameworks.

<a id="strategic-objectives-and-indicators-2"></a>

### Strategic Objectives and Indicators

The CFC’s Strategic Priorities 2026-2030 establish four strategic pillars that guide the organisation’s activities and performance over the planning period.

Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Peoples: Leadership, Culture and Cultural Integrity

The CFC will strengthen partnerships and shared decision-making with Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples and communities, embed Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander-led storytelling and cultural practices across programming and interpretation, and build organisational cultural integrity through workforce capability and policy alignment.

Key indicators include:

Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander representation in programming and interpretation;

participation of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander artists, knowledge holders and communities;

cultural competency and cultural integrity measures; and

evidence of partner confidence, trust and engagement.

Connection: Building Belonging Through Creativity

The CFC will deliver inclusive, relevant and high-quality cultural experiences that foster connection, wellbeing and participation across diverse communities. The organisation will continue to strengthen access to collections, heritage and stories through physical, digital and place-based experiences.

Key indicators include:

growth and diversity of visitors and participation;

audience and visitor experience measures;

access to collections and digital engagement; and

engagement with artists, schools, communities and partners.

Growth: Transforming Canberra’s Cultural Landscape

The CFC will support a significant expansion of Canberra’s cultural capacity through delivery of the expanded Canberra Theatre Centre and Lyric Theatre, strengthened precinct integration and increased cultural activity across the Civic and Cultural District.

The organisation will focus on operational readiness, ambitious programming, audience growth, partnership development and strengthening Canberra’s national cultural profile.

Key indicators include:

operational readiness milestones for the Lyric Theatre and expanded Canberra Theatre Centre;

growth and diversification of audiences;

increased scale and diversity of productions, exhibitions and events;

growth in memberships, partnerships and philanthropic engagement; and

increased national recognition and cultural impact.

Organisational Capability: Strengthening Foundations for the Future

The CFC will continue to strengthen workforce capability, leadership, systems, governance and operational resilience to support sustainable organisational growth and cultural leadership.

Key indicators include:

workforce capability, engagement and retention;

operational efficiency and systems integration;

revenue diversification and financial sustainability; and

governance, audit, risk and work health and safety performance.

<a id="output-11-cultural-facilities-management"></a>

##### Output 1.1: Cultural Facilities Management

CFC manages a number of the ACT’s major cultural assets, comprising:

the Canberra Theatre Centre;

Canberra Museum and Gallery (CMAG), including the Nolan Collection;

Lanyon Homestead;

Calthorpes’ House;

Mugga Mugga; and

Albert Hall

Through its activities at these venues, the CFC provides cultural services across the performing arts, the visual arts, social history and cultural heritage management, by:

providing performing arts presentations, exhibitions, education programs, community programs and other events; and

conserving and interpreting the historic sites that it manages.

##### Output 1.1: Cultural Facilities Management

|   | 2025-26Targets | 2025-26Estimated Outcome | 2026-27Targets |
|---|---|---|---|
| Estimated number of visitors/patrons to CFC facilities/programs 1 | 419,000 | 729,928 | 544,685 |
| Number of exhibitions at facilities managed by CFC 2 | 27 | 25 | 22 |
| Number of education and community programs provided by CFC 3 | 300 | 370 | 600 |

Accountability Indicator Descriptions and Variance Commentary:

The increase in the number of visitors/patrons to CFC facilities/programs in the 2025-26 Estimated Outcome compared with the 2025-26 Target is primarily driven by higher attendance at touring and outreach activities delivered by Canberra Museum and Gallery and Historic Places, including visits to collections held offsite. The 2026-27 Target is established based on anticipated visitation across the Canberra Theatre Centre, Canberra Museum and Gallery, Historic Places and touring exhibitions.

The slightly lower 2025-26 estimated outcome compared to the 2025-26 Target is due to the extension of the Taglietti: Life in Design exhibition at request of ACT Heritage to align with ACT Heritage Festival and theme of “Mid-Century Heritage”, and a cancelled touring exhibition.

Target for 2026-27 is higher than the 2025-26 estimated outcome due to an anticipated increase in the ability to offer more public and education programs supported by a new café & catering services operator.

The decrease in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the 2025-26 Target is due to increased visitor/patron numbers as explained in Note 1. The increase in the 2026-27 Targets is due to higher government appropriation to support the operational readiness of the Lyric Theatre.

The decrease in the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to total revenue for the year included gains from the asset transfer of Albert Hall from Infrastructure Canberra. The decrease in the 2026-27 Target is due to higher government appropriation to support the operational readiness of the Lyric Theatre.

<a id="strategic-asset-management-plan"></a>

#### Strategic Asset Management Plan

The CFC is the custodian and manager of a number of public building assets in the ACT. The assets are culturally significant to the ACT community, and include venues of historical importance, public theatres and galleries. The building portfolio is diverse, including income generating non-commercial and heritage assets. The managed premises range from domestic housing to major civic buildings.

The CFC’s Strategic Asset Management Plan forms the basis of its strategic asset planning, including the development of proposals for capital works funding. A comprehensive review and update of the Plan was undertaken in 2010-11 to cover the 20 year timeframe from 2011-2030. A further update of the Plan for the Canberra Theatre Centre was completed in 2016-17, while further updates for Canberra Museum and Gallery and the ACT Historic Places were completed in 2017-18.


<a id="monitoring-and-reporting-3"></a>

###### Monitoring and Reporting

The CFC will satisfy the requirements of the Chief Minister’s Annual Reports Directions.  The CFC Annual Report will, amongst other things, report against the requirements of this Statement of Intent.

The Financial Management Act 1996 authorises the Treasurer to obtain financial and other statements from CFC for a stated period including annual, quarterly and monthly reporting.


<a id="annual-reporting-3"></a>

#### Annual Reporting

As part of preparations for end of year reporting, CMTEDD will advise the dates when the following documents are required at the CMTEDD and at the Auditor General's Office.

Certified financial statements.

Management discussion and analysis.

A full and accurate set of audited financial records for the preceding financial year in the form requested.

Consolidation packs relating to the annual financial statements, draft and final.


<a id="financial-arrangements-4"></a>

###### Financial Arrangements

During 2026-27, the CFC’s main focus in terms of financial management is to continue to develop the organisation’s capacity to deliver artistic services of outstanding quality and to prepare the organisation for the significant change in operations as a result of the construction of the Lyric Theatre. Under the CFC’s strategic plan, this focus will include income diversification including potential expanded revenue generating projects at its venues and positioning itself for its ongoing leadership role of driving growth in the contribution of the arts to the ACT economy.

The deficits that are projected for the CFC’s operating results are due to it incurring large unfunded depreciation expenses on its substantial asset holdings. The CFC’s approach to managing its finances is to seek to achieve operating outcomes equal to or better than the projected deficits.

<a id="icon-water-limited"></a>

## ICON WATER LIMITED


<a id="purpose-6"></a>

### Purpose

Icon Water provides water and sewerage services to the ACT community and bulk water to Queanbeyan.  Icon Water also holds a 50 per cent investment in the ActewAGL joint venture (the energy investment) which provides electricity and gas to the ACT and surrounding region.

The Territory-owned Corporations Act 1990 (TOC Act) identifies four equally important objectives for Icon Water.

To operate at least as efficiently as any comparable business.

To maximise the sustainable return to the Territory on its investments, in accordance with the performance targets in Icon Water’s Statement of Corporate Intent.

To show a sense of social responsibility by having regard to the interests of the community in which we operate, and by trying to accommodate and encourage those interests.

Where our activities affect the environment, to effectively integrate environmental and economic considerations in decision-making processes.

These main objectives provide the foundation upon which we conducted strategic planning and identified our vision and objectives.


<a id="2026-27-priorities-3"></a>

### 2026-27 Priorities

Icon Water’s work will be guided by four key strategic outcomes:

safe and reliable services;

value for our community;

our people at their best; and

a resilient water future.

Achievement of these objectives is underpinned by strategies as follows:

Safe and reliable services: We have been entrusted with providing reliable wastewater services for Canberra, and quality water for Canberra and neighbouring communities. We need to be vigilant in upholding this responsibility for future generations, while also working to create great experiences for customers. We recognise the specific customer needs of the development industry for clarity and responsiveness.

While our network is performing reliably, we are strengthening how we manage and use data so we can better respond to ageing assets and the increasing complexity that comes with growth. During 2026-27 we will build on our ability to predict emerging issues, improve the quality of the information we rely on and protect the personal data entrusted to us. These improvements will help us maintain the trust of our customers and ensure we can respond effectively when issues arise.

Value for our community: As a government-owned utility, we have a particular responsibility to create value for our community through affordable services and timely and cost-effective long-term investment in assets. Value is also about how we show up in our community through our core services – including how we recognise and protect the cultural heritage of our community and contribute to social and economic development in the ACT and region. Demonstrating value for our community is key to us maintaining trust.

Affordability remains a priority for our community and is front of mind as we prepare our 2028‑33 price proposal. We are taking a disciplined approach to balancing long-term investment with the impact on customers, and a focus on avoiding sharp or unexpected price increases. We will keep supporting customers who need assistance with their bills and ensure our decisions are guided by the expectations of our community. We will continue to work with our energy investment partners to support the ACT Government’s goal of net zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2045. Our role in managing these investments is an important part of how we deliver value for our community.

Our people at their best: Our strategic aspirations can only be achieved through the empowerment of our people. The way we organise ourselves, the systems and tools we provide, and the culture we create all play a crucial role in enabling our people to enjoy their work and make their best contribution.

In 2026-27, we will further strengthen our leadership and workforce planning frameworks, support the professional growth of our people and embed our new operating model. We are committed to providing a safe, inclusive and supportive workplace where our people can do their best work and feel proud of the contribution they make to our community.

A resilient water future: Achieving a resilient water future speaks directly to our vision of ‘creating a sustainable water legacy for our community’. It involves making wise, long-term decisions that secure water for Canberra and the region, consider the whole water cycle, and protect the environment for future generations.

Canberra is growing and our climate is becoming more variable. These challenges reinforce the importance of integrated management and long-term planning for both water security and wastewater services. In 2026-27 we will continue to progress the work that will inform future investment decisions to secure our water future, including supply options, demand management and the long-term pathway for our wastewater system. As part of our response to these challenges, together with our major projects partner, we are progressing the upgrade and modernisation of the ACT’s main wastewater treatment plant to ensure it can meet future service needs for our community.

Icon Water’s 2026-27 Budget forecasts are subject to potentially significant variation arising from factors outside Icon Water’s control.  These factors include the following:

Regulatory – revenue targets in the 2026-27 Budget and forward estimates are based on the Independent Competition & Regulatory Commission (ICRC) 2023-28 pricing decision and have been adjusted for the annual price adjustments to date. For 2026-27 to 2027-28 years, there is an estimated allowance for pass through revenue adjustments relating to implementation costs that have been triggered due to a change in legislation (e.g. unit metering and security of critical infrastructure).

Climatic conditions – average projection weather patterns with a high dam level baseline, antecedent catchment conditions and short-term climate drivers have been assumed in determining the budget estimates.  Variability in climatic conditions will result in material variations to Icon Water’s profitability through altered water consumption patterns, pumping and treatment costs. Extreme weather events may also result in financial losses and impact our service levels.

Icon Water operations – water and sewer operations may be affected by geopolitical risks that contribute to fuel and energy price volatility, disruptions to global supply chains, shortages or delays in critical materials and equipment, and broader inflationary pressures. These factors may affect operating costs, capital delivery costs and timeframes and service resilience across the water industry.

The energy investment – the profitability of the energy investment may be affected by external factors including climatic conditions, feed in tariff fluctuations, energy price volatility, regulatory outcomes, and flow on impacts to achieve net zero emissions by 2045. Growing solar exports have a material impact on load requirement resulting in greater challenges for both the retail business through increased variability of wholesale prices and the distribution business through greater required investment to maintain grid stability.

Contributed assets - Contributed assets include gifted assets and capital contribution funded assets. The annual value of contributed assets can vary significantly for both water and sewerage, and the energy business (ActewAGL). The gifted assets revenue is dependent upon the timing of land releases, construction timeframes and the completion period for acceptance into the respective network. Icon Water’s estimate for gifted assets from developers is based on the historical four-year average and the latest available Suburban Land Agency forecasts.

Economic Conditions and Borrowings– Icon Water will need to source new debt and/or refinance existing debt in relation to both water and sewerage and energy investment businesses, which will be subject to market interest rates. Icon Water uses estimates for interest rates, pay rises and CPI assumptions in the Budget and forward estimates which small changes in can have material impact of profitability.

There is an assumption that debt will be introduced into the ActewAGL Distribution partnership during the SCI time horizon. The introduction and timing of debt remains uncertain, in the absence of its introduction the prevailing funding mechanisms will continue to apply.

Additionally, construction and project management costs are especially vulnerable to escalation, with inflation amplifying the costs of raw materials, equipment, and contractor rates. These impacts can lead to substantial increases in capital investment outlays, potentially requiring project re-scoping, reprioritisation, or additional funding to achieve strategic objectives.

Impairment – the budget assumes no impairment or revaluation adjustment of assets.  Significant impairment or revaluation adjustments will impact the return on investment.

intangible assets: the increase of $17.369 million in the 2026-27 Budget mainly relates to increased investment in intangible assets related to the uplift required in line Security of Critical Infrastructure legislation.

current and non-current interest-bearing liabilities: the increase of $418.186 million in the 2026‑27 Budget mainly relates to an increase in borrowings required to fund the investment program.

income tax payable and deferred tax liability: the increase of $61.742 million in the 2025‑26 estimated outcome mainly relates to the difference in timing of income tax expense recognition under tax legislation compared to accounting standard requirements. <a id="independent-competition-and-regulatory-commission-statement-of-intent"></a>

## INDEPENDENT COMPETITION AND REGULATORY COMMISSION – STATEMENT OF INTENT

The Independent Competition and Regulatory Commission (the Commission) is a Territory Authority established under the Independent Competition and Regulatory Commission Act 1997 (ICRC Act). The Commission is constituted under the ICRC Act by one or more standing commissioners and any associated commissioners appointed for particular purposes. Commissioners are statutory appointments. The current Commissioner is Senior Commissioner Richard Owens who has direct responsibility for delivery of the outcomes that are foreshadowed in this Statement of Intent.

This Statement of Intent for 2026­27 has been prepared in accordance with Section 61 of the Financial Management Act 1996.

The responsible Minister, Mr Chris Steel MLA, was consulted during the preparation of the Statement of Intent.

The Statement of Intent, which focuses on the 2026­27 Budget year, has been developed in the context of a four year forward planning horizon to be incorporated, as far as practicable, into the Independent Competition and Regulatory Commission strategic and business planning processes.

The Independent Competition and Regulatory Commission 2026­27 Statement of Intent has been agreed between:

Richard Owens

Senior Commissioner

Chris Steel MLA

Treasurer

This page deliberately left blank


<a id="independent-competition-and-regulatory-commission"></a>

## INDEPENDENT COMPETITION AND REGULATORY COMMISSION


<a id="purpose-7"></a>

### Purpose

The Commission has responsibilities for a broad range of competition, regulation and consumer protection matters. The Commission is responsible under the Independent Competition and Regulatory Commission Act 1997 (ICRC Act) for:

regulating and advising government about pricing and other matters for monopoly, near‑monopoly and ministerially declared regulated industries;

providing advice on competitive neutrality complaints and government-regulated activities, and on other industry-related matters referred to the Commission; and

arbitrating infrastructure access disputes under the ICRC Act.

The Commission’s objectives are set out in sections 7 and 19L of the ICRC Act and section 3 of the Utilities Act 2000 (Utilities Act). Those objectives are:

promoting effective competition in the interests of consumers;

facilitating an appropriate balance between efficiency, environmental and social considerations;

ensuring non-discriminatory access to monopoly and near-monopoly infrastructure; and

when making a price direction in a regulated industry, promoting the efficient investment in, and efficient operation and use of regulated services for the long-term interests of consumers in relation to price, quality, safety, reliability and security of the service.

The Utilities Act provides for the licensing of electricity, natural gas, water and sewerage utility services. In meeting its responsibilities under this legislation, the Commission’s objectives are to:

encourage the provision of safe, reliable, efficient and high quality utility services at reasonable prices;

minimise the potential for misuse of monopoly power and promote competition in the provision of utility services;

encourage long term investment, growth and employment in utility services; and

protect the interests of consumers.


<a id="nature-and-scope-of-activities-5"></a>

### Nature and Scope of Activities


<a id="activities-to-meet-legislated-objectives"></a>

#### Activities to Meet Legislated Objectives


<a id="retail-electricity-pricing-arrangements"></a>

##### Retail Electricity Pricing Arrangements

From 1 July 2012, some responsibilities for regulating retail electricity were transferred to the Australian Energy Regulator (AER) but responsibility for technical regulation, complaints handling, and retail electricity pricing remained with state and territory governments. In line with ACT government policy and the regulated industry provisions in the ICRC Act, the Commission determines retail prices for the supply of electricity to small customers on standard retail contracts who consume less than 100MWh of electricity over any period of 12 consecutive months.

On 1 June 2023, the Commission received the terms of reference from the ACT Government to determine a price direction for standing offer prices for the supply of electricity by ActewAGL Retail (ActewAGL) to small customers for the period 1 July 2024 to 30 June 2027.

In determining the price direction, the Commission engaged in broad consultation with the public, the sector and other interested parties. The Commission published issues and draft papers seeking submissions, undertook a public survey and held a public forum.

On 23 May 2024, the Commission released its final decision and price direction on the regulated retail electricity prices to apply from 1 July 2024 to 30 June 2027.

In accordance with the price direction, on 25 May 2026 the Commission announced the maximum percentage change of the prices for regulated retail electricity prices in 2026-27.


<a id="water-and-sewerage-services-pricing-arrangements"></a>

##### Water and Sewerage Services Pricing Arrangements

As a regulated industry under the ICRC Act, prices for water and sewerage services in the ACT are determined by the Commission.

On 10 December 2021 the Commission received an industry reference to determine a price direction for water and sewerage services for 1 July 2023 to 30 June 2028. The Commission released its final report and price direction 2023-28 for water and sewerage services on 1 May 2023.

In accordance with the price direction, on 2 June 2026 the Commission announced the annual price adjustment with an allowed increase of the prices for water and sewerage services in 2026-27.

On 5 November 2024 the Commission announced reviews of the Water and Sewerage Capital Contribution Code, including charges by Icon Water for developers, as well as a sewerage and trade waste charging. The Capital Contribution Code was finalised on 1 September 2025, while an issues paper was released for the Sewerage Tariff and Liquid Trade Waste review on 6 November 2025. The Commission will progress this work program during the current regulatory period so that any changes can be incorporated into the next price review.


<a id="utilities-regulatory-regime"></a>

##### Utilities Regulatory Regime

The Commission has a statutory responsibility to ensure utilities licensed in the Territory comply with their obligations under the Utilities Act, licence conditions, and industry codes. Collectively these give power to the objectives under the ICRC and Utilities Acts for the provision of safe, reliable, efficient and high-quality utility services while protecting the long-term interests of consumers. The ACT licensing regime applies to electricity generation, electricity and gas transmission, distribution and connection services and water and sewerage services. The Commission assesses licence applications, maintains the licensing regime, and monitors and reports on licensed utilities’ compliance with licence conditions. On 6 October 2023 the Commission determined new application fees attached to utility licence applications.

The Commission regularly reviews and updates utility licences, codes and guidelines to ensure they remain appropriate, considering current and emerging consumer issues and utility industry developments

In February 2021 the Commission updated the utility licence terms and conditions to improve clarity, streamline reporting requirements, and reflect changes in the regulatory framework. The update reduces regulatory burden and ensures the regulatory regime remains current and continues to meet the objectives of the Utilities Act.

In October 2024 the commission updated the licence application package to reflect changes in the energy market and updated regulatory framework.

In March 2025 Icon Water’s licence was varied to reflect the change in regulatory framework regarding firefighting standards and the Water Metering Code following the introduction of the Utilities (Technical Regulation) (Water and Sewerage Code) Approval 2024.

In June 2026 the Commission commenced a review of the Consumer Protection Code considering whether protections are appropriate and meaningful, whether the Code is accessible, clear, and enforceable; and manage overlaps with the national regulatory framework.

The Commission monitors compliance of each licensed utility with its regulatory obligations through a Utility Licence Annual Report. Each utility is required to report on the exercise of its functions under the Utilities Act; and compliance with the conditions of its licence. The Commission assesses the material for compliance with the relevant obligations and conditions and the results are set out in the Commission’s monitoring report.

Following the 2023 amendment to the Climate Change and Greenhouse Gas Reduction Regulation 2011, the gas distributor must provide an annual report on new gas connections to the Commission as part of its licence conditions. The Commission publishes this information in its annual monitoring report.

The 2024-25 report was released on 18 February 2026 and included reporting on payments made against the Guaranteed Service Levels in the Consumer Protection Code. This report also included a section on the compliance of electricity retailers.

Under the industry code covering capital contributions for water and sewerage infrastructure upgrades, which took effect from 1 January 2018, the Commission reviews the contributions charge and associated infrastructure projects annually. Upon application from Icon Water, the Commission also considers updates to the precinct map. On 2 June 2026 the Commission approved the precinct map updates and the level of the charge.

The Commission determined the ACT Retail Electricity (Transparency and Comparability) Code 2021. The code makes it easier for small businesses and residential customers to compare electricity offers and save money on their bills. The code also requires retailers to provide clear advice when making their offers, which helps protect and inform consumers. The Commission monitors compliance with the code through annual reporting and proactive monitoring.

In May 2024, the ACT Legislative Assembly amended the ICRC Act to provide additional civil enforcement powers to the Commission, with the changes coming into effect in December 2024. Following consultation with industry stakeholders, the Commission published its compliance and enforcement strategy on 10 December 2024, providing guidance to the industry on how the Commission will use these new powers.


<a id="advice-to-government-on-industry-related-matters"></a>

##### Advice to Government on Industry-Related Matters

The Commission provides advice to the ACT Government on matters related to an industry or to industries in general under terms of reference given under the ICRC Act.


<a id="competition-policy"></a>

##### Competition Policy

The Commission provides advice on competition policy matters as required by the ACT Government under the ICRC Act. The Commission also investigates and reports on competitive neutrality complaints as they are received, consistent with the National Competition Policy Agreements and the ACT Competitive Neutrality Policy.

In the terms of reference for the retail electricity price investigation the ACT Government asked the Commission to ensure the methodology used does not unduly disadvantage those who do not actively engage in the energy market, while balancing the competitiveness of the retail electricity market. In response the Commission assessed the level of competition in the retail electricity market in the ACT historically as well as against national benchmarks. Our assessment is included in the final report, report 5 of 2024.

Following the price determination the Commission has released ACT retail electricity market monitoring reports annually, with the 2025 report released on 4 December 2025.

The Commission also provided advice to the ACT Government in updating its Competitive Neutrality Policy in response to the National Competition Policy revitalisation in 2025-26.


<a id="risks-3"></a>

#### Risks

Effective risk management is essential to achieving the Commission’s purpose, satisfying stakeholder expectations, and ensuring compliance with legislation and regulations governing the Commission’s operations.

The Commission has developed an organisational risk management plan and a risk register to identify and manage risks across the organisation. The risk register is reviewed and updated regularly. In addition, project-specific plans are developed, monitored and updated during each project. The Commission has identified three main areas of risk for achieving its objectives – operational, organisational and financial.

The Commission has strategies to manage the operational risks arising from the cyclical nature of its work. These include smoothing out, where possible, workload peaks and troughs to create a more stable workload and reduce pressure on staff, given the deadlines associated with the Commission’s decision-making responsibilities. Operational risk is also managed by appropriate training and supervision of staff and adequate internal policies, processes and systems. External risks, such as non-performance under contracts, are managed through careful evaluation of risk factors and implementation of policies and procedures to manage those risks.

The Commission operates a hybrid office arrangement. Over 2025-26, the Commission maintained suitable policies and processes to manage work health and safety risks and has a business continuity plan should these risks escalate. These actions ensured the Commission continued to operate effectively and met all statutory deadlines while protecting the health and safety of its staff and stakeholders.

Organisational risk arises from the complexity of the Commission’s work and its small and specialised staff. The Commission faces the ongoing risk of loss of organisational capacity and corporate knowledge caused by challenges in attracting and retaining staff with the required specialised skills and experience. The Commission has strategies to manage organisational risk, including training and development of staff, provision of an attractive and rewarding working environment, effective records and knowledge management, and business continuity and project planning.

As a largely user-funded organisation, the Commission’s financial risk mainly arises from inadequate cash reserves to meet the Commission’s financial commitments. The Commission has appropriate risk management procedures to identify and manage financial risk, including actively monitoring credit risk in areas such as credit cards and other receivables appropriate to its nature, scale and size.


<a id="2026-27-priorities-and-next-three-financial-years-4"></a>

### 2026-27 Priorities and Next Three Financial Years

The Commission’s strategic and operational priorities for 2026­27 include:

undertaking a price investigation into retail electricity supplied to small customers on standard retail contracts for the regulatory period from 1 July 2027 and, in consultation with electricity retailers, the community and other stakeholders, determining prices that meet our objectives as set out in section 7 and 19L of the ICRC Act and section 3 of the Utilities Act;

overseeing the annual adjustment, including for pass-through events, required by the price determination for regulated water and sewerage services 2023-28;

commencing a price investigation into water and sewerage services for the regulatory period from 1 July 2028 and, in consultation with Icon Water, the community and other stakeholders, determining prices that meet our objectives as set out in section 7 and 19L of the ICRC Act and section 3 of the Utilities Act;

completing the review of the Sewerage Tariffs and Liquid Trade Waste Charging, allowing any recommendations to be incorporated in the price investigation listed above;

completing the review of the Consumer Protection Code;

overseeing the regulatory regime established under the Utilities Act, including licensing, industry code development and updates and compliance monitoring and enforcement;

continue to develop other internal and external guidance to allow the Commission to use its enforcement powers, should they be necessary;

reporting on utility compliance and performance, benchmarking against the Guaranteed Service Levels defined in the Consumer Protection Code as well as electricity retailers’ compliance with the Retail Electricity Code;

recovering annual utility licence fees and administering the annual energy industry levy;

responding to any new terms of reference received from the ACT Government for advice on industry-related matters; and

responding to the Government’s requirements for advice on competition policy matters and discharging the Commission’s statutory role in competitive neutrality complaints handling.

The strategic and operational issues to be pursued in the following three financial years include:

completing a price investigation into water and sewerage services for the regulatory period from 1 July 2028 and, in consultation with Icon Water, the community and other stakeholders, determining prices that meet our objectives as set out in the ICRC Act and the Utilities Act;

overseeing any annual adjustments, including for pass-through events, required by a price determination for retail electricity supplied to small customers on standard retail contracts in accordance with the terms of reference;

overseeing any annual adjustments, including for pass-through events, required by a price determination for water and sewerage services in accordance with the terms of reference;

overseeing the regulatory regime established under the Utilities Act, including licensing, amending existing licences to take account of any legislative changes, industry code development and updates, recovering annual utility licence fees, administering the energy levy;

responding to any terms of reference received from the ACT Government for advice on industry-related matters; and

responding to the Government’s requests for advice on competition policy matters and discharging the Commission’s statutory role in competitive neutrality complaints handling.

<a id="operating-statement-5"></a>

##### Operating Statement

controlled recurrent payments: the decrease of $0.150 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome reflects a reduced energy levy for the year as the commission will not undertake an electricity reset in 2026-27. Instead, the Commission will commence a price investigation into retail electricity supplied to small customers on standard retail contracts for the regulatory period from 1 July 2027, which is recognised in the sales of goods and services from contracts with customers below.

taxes, licences, fees, and fines: the decrease of $0.119 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome reflects lower fees as the Commission commences a pricing investigation to determine the regulated prices of water and sewerage services in the regulatory period from 1 July 2028. This activity is recognised in the sales of goods and services from contracts with customers below.

sale of goods and services from contracts with customers: the increase of $1.302 million is attributable to the recovery of costs for the investigation into, and price direction determination for, retail electricity supplied to small customers on standard retail contracts for the regulatory period from 1 July 2027. It also includes recovery of costs for commencing the investigation into, and price direction determination for, regulated water and sewerage services for the regulatory period from 1 July 2028.

supplies and services: the increase of $0.354 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is primarily driven by anticipated consultant costs associated with the investigations into, and price direction determinations for, both the retail electricity supplied to small customers on standard retail contracts for the regulatory period from 1 July 2027 and the regulated water and sewerage services for the regulatory period from 1 July 2028.

operating result: the increase of $0.504 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is due to the investigations into, and price direction determinations for, both the retail electricity supplied to small customers on standard retail contracts for the regulatory period from 1 July 2027 and the regulated water and sewerage services for the regulatory period from 1 July 2028.

<a id="lifetime-care-and-support-fund"></a>

## LIFETIME CARE AND SUPPORT FUND


<a id="purpose-8"></a>

### Purpose

The Lifetime Care and Support Fund (LTCS fund) was established under the Lifetime Care and Support (Catastrophic Injuries) Act 2014 (LTCS Act) and commenced operations on 1 July 2014. The LTCS fund reflects the financial operations of the Lifetime Care and Support Scheme (LTCS Scheme).

The LTCS Scheme provides on-going treatment and care on a no-fault basis to people who have been catastrophically injured as a result of a motor accident in the Australian Capital Territory (ACT) or a workplace accident in the course of their private sector employment in the ACT.

The LTCS Scheme is funded by two levies: a levy on all Motor Accident Injuries (MAI) insurance policies and a levy on private sector workers’ compensation insurers and self-insurers.


<a id="2026-27-priorities-4"></a>

### 2026-27 Priorities

The LTCS fund will be pursuing several strategic and operational matters in 2026-27, including:

issuing/amending guidelines and determinations when required under the LTCS Act;

assessing future funding requirements and levy setting;

collecting feedback from participants on their expectations and experience with the LTCS Scheme; and

undertaking investments in accordance with the Fund’s Investment Strategy.


<a id="estimated-employment-levels-6"></a>

### Estimated Employment Levels

The functions of the LTCS Scheme are performed by officers of the Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate (CMTEDD) and those employees are included in CMTEDD’s FTE levels. The LTCS fund reimburses CMTEDD for the salary and superannuation expenses associated with the staff allocated to carry out the LTCS Scheme’s functions.


<a id="strategic-objectives-and-indicators-3"></a>

### Strategic Objectives and Indicators


<a id="strategic-objective-1"></a>

#### Strategic Objective 1

Achievement of Investment Return Objective

Strategic Indicator 1:  To achieve a long-term investment return of CPI + 2.5 per cent per annum.1

Note:

This indicator measures the annual LTCS fund’s portfolio investment return since inception. The objective is to achieve an investment return of CPI + 2.5 per cent per annum over the long-term.


<a id="output-classes-territorial"></a>

### Output Classes (Territorial)

<a id="ebt-11-lifetime-care-and-support-fund"></a>

##### EBT 1.1: Lifetime Care and Support Fund

The LTCS fund reflects the operation of the LTCS Scheme to provide on-going treatment and care to people who have been catastrophically injured as a result of a motor accident in the ACT or workplace accident in the course of private sector employment in the ACT.

The key outputs to be delivered in 2026-27 include:

deciding the eligibility of applicants to the LTCS Scheme in accordance with Part 4 of the LTCS Act and the LTCS Guidelines;

assessing the treatment and care needs of participants in the LTCS Scheme in accordance with Part 5 of the LTCS Act;

paying all reasonable expenses incurred by or on behalf of an injured person in relation to the injured person’s assessed treatment and care needs in accordance with Part 6 and Part 9 of the LTCS Act;

monitoring service delivery and participant outcomes, including collecting feedback from participants on their expectations and experience with the LTCS Scheme;

assessing future funding requirements and levy setting;

reviewing and updating the LTCS Guidelines;

undertaking investments in accordance with the Fund’s Investment Strategy; and

determining LTCS levies in accordance with Part 10 of the LTCS Act.

| Provision of LTCS Scheme in the ACT |   |   |   |
| LTCS levies determined during the fourth quarter | 2 | 2 | 2 |
| Decision on applicant eligibility for participation in LTCS Scheme within 10 business days of receiving all relevant information. | 85% | 100% | 85% |

Note(s):

The levy determinations must be made in accordance with section 84 of the LTCS Act. The determinations need to occur during the fourth quarter of the financial year so that they are available for commencement on 1 July. The levy determination for the 2026-27 contribution period for motor vehicles was notified on the Legislation Register in April 2026 and for private workers' compensation insurers and self-insurers was notified in May 2026.

This indicator requires the LTCS Fund to assess and report on its performance against the target that 85 per cent of decisions on (interim and lifetime) applicant eligibility for participation in the LTCS Scheme are made within 10 business days of receiving all relevant information. Relevant information is all information required to assess eligibility and includes accident and medical information. All decisions on applicant eligibility are expected to be made within 10 business days of receiving relevant information in 2025-26.


<a id="financial-statements-territorial-2"></a>

### Financial Statements – Territorial

Presentational changes have been made to streamline the financial statements and the 2026‑27 Budget column also reflects this change. These changes will be consistent with future annual financial statements and ensure comparability of the annual financial statements with the budget estimates as required under section 27 of the Financial Management Act 1996.

Transition to AASB 17 Insurance Contracts

Currently the LTCS fund estimates the provision for the future treatment and care costs of the LTCS Scheme participants based on the requirements of AASB 137 Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets. From 1 July 2026 the LTCS fund will adopt AASB 17 Insurance Contracts. The application of AASB 17 changes the measurement, recognition and disclosure of the Fund’s expenses and provision associated with the costs of the LTCS Scheme participants.

The 2026­27 Budget is the first budget prepared reflecting the 2026-27 and forward years estimates under AASB 17.

The LTCS fund will adopt the full retrospective approach on transition. However, the 2026-27 Budget does not restate prior year comparative estimates for 2025-26. The accounting impact arising on transition is reflected as an adjustment to the opening accumulated funds as at 1 July 2026 in the Fund’s Statement of Changes in Equity on Behalf of the Territory.

While the 2026­27 and forward years estimates are calculated based on the requirements of AASB 17, the presentation continues to follow the existing budget framework. Accordingly, the classification and presentation of insurance contracts in the 2026-27 Budget may differ from the 2026­27 financial statements, which will be prepared under AASB 17. As a result, the 2026‑27 Budget presented in the 2026-27 financial statements will be different to the existing budget framework. There is no impact on the overall financial estimates because of this presentational difference.

The transition to AASB 17 does not impact the operation of the LTCS Scheme.

<a id="notes-to-the-territorial-budget-statements-2"></a>

#### Notes to the Territorial Budget Statements

Significant variations are as follows.


<a id="statement-of-income-and-expenses-on-behalf-of-the-territory-2"></a>

##### Statement of Income and Expenses on behalf of the Territory

investment revenue: is associated with distributions from investments with the Territory Banking Account (TBA):

the decrease of $2.979 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects the level of available distribution income from TBA; and

the increase of $3.807 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is due to the estimated additional investment during the 2025-26 financial year, as well as the distribution revenue being based on estimated distributable income and the long-term investment return objective.

other income: is associated with the two LTCS levies - a levy relating to motor accident injuries and a levy relating to work injuries.

LTCS levies for 2026-27 are determined in the last quarter of 2025-26 by the LTCS Commissioner. The LTCS levy relating to motor accident injuries for the 2026-27 contribution period is $110.4 (2025-26: $110.4) for a 12-month ACT MAI policy charged proportionately. The LTCS levy for vehicles registered under the ACT’s Veteran, Vintage and Historic (VVH) Registration Scheme including the modified historic vehicles, remains at 20 per cent of the full year LTCS levy. The LTCS levy relating to private sector work injuries for the 2026-27 contribution period is $8.123 million (2025-26: $8.123 million).

Sufficiency of the LTCS levies to fund the scheme’s estimated liabilities is reassessed before the start of a financial year by the LTCS Commissioner based on advice of an independent actuary.

net gain/(loss) on investments with the Territory Banking Account (TBA): the increase of $2.123 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is due to the expected investment return for the 2025-26 financial year being higher than assumed.

other expenses: are associated with the provision for estimated future treatment and care costs of ACT LTCS Scheme participants based on actuarial projections and the unwinding of the discount rate associated with the estimation of the present value of those future costs.

The 2026-27 Budget includes unwinding of discount expenses ($12.979 million) attributable to the change in liabilities as they become one year closer to payment:

the decrease of $33.052 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due to the decrease in provision for ACT participants’ treatment and care costs primarily resulting from lower than budgeted new participants entering the ACT LTCS Scheme in 2025-26, improvements in the injury severity of a number of existing participants; partially offset by increases resulting from changes in economic assumptions; and

the increase of $34.907 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated is mainly due to lower provision for ACT participants’ treatment and care costs in 2025-26 (discussed above). The increase is also due to the increase in provision associated with the estimated new participants expected to enter the ACT LTCS Scheme in 2026-27, and due to the increase in the estimated unwinding of discount expenses resulting from higher opening liabilities. The 2026-27 expenses estimates are based on the requirements of AASB 17 Insurance Contracts.

Volatility is expected each year regarding the number of new participants entering the LTCS Scheme and their estimated lifetime treatment and care expenses.


<a id="statement-of-assets-and-liabilities-on-behalf-of-the-territory-2"></a>

##### Statement of Assets and Liabilities on behalf of the Territory

cash: the increase of $1.682 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly associated with maintaining sufficient cash balance to allow payment of all current liabilities when they fall due.

receivables: the increase of $1.074 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025‑26 estimated outcome is mainly associated with the expected higher receivable for distribution on investments held with the TBA for the quarter ended 30 June 2027 compared to 30 June 2026, and the higher levy relating to motor accident injuries receivable for the month of June 2027.

other assets: are associated with the reserve held by the New South Wales Lifetime Treatment and Care Authority (NSW LTCSA) to fund the estimated ACT LTCS Scheme participants’ treatment and care payments for the next quarter until reimbursement from the LTCS fund. This is under the arrangement that commenced during 2015-16 for NSW LTCSA to administer the ACT LTCS Scheme. During 2025-26 the reserve was increased from $2.270 million to $2.500 million due to the increase in the ACT participants’ treatment and care payments resulting from an increase in the number of participants in the ACT LTCS Scheme.

investments:

the increase of $9.138 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due to the higher opening balance, higher than anticipated additional investments during the year and higher gains on investments held with the TBA during 2025­26; and

the increase of $43.266 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to the $38.492 million estimated additional investment of collected funds to provide for estimated new ACT participants. Funds are collected to provide for the estimated whole of life costs of participants in the year they are accepted into the ACT LTCS Scheme.

other provisions (Current and Non-Current):

the decrease of $37.144 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due to the lower provision for ACT participants’ treatment and care costs mainly resulting from the lower than budgeted new participants entering the ACT LTCS Scheme in 2025­26, improvements in the injury severity of a number of existing participants; partially offset by increases resulting from changes in economic assumptions; and

the increase of $47.762 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to the estimated lifetime costs of the estimated new participants entering the LTCS Scheme in 2026-27 and the estimated unwinding of discount expenses in 2026-27. The 2026-27 provision estimate is based on the requirements of AASB 17 Insurance Contracts.


<a id="statement-of-changes-in-equity-on-behalf-of-the-territory"></a>

##### Statement of Changes in Equity on behalf of the Territory

Net Effect of Change in Accounting Policy: is associated with the impact of transitioning to AASB 17 Insurance Contracts from 1 July 2026.
