<a id="motor-accident-injuries-commission-statement-of-intent"></a>

## MOTOR ACCIDENT INJURIES COMMISSION – STATEMENT OF INTENT

The Motor Accident Injuries Commission (MAI Commission) is a Territory Authority established under the Motor Accident Injuries Act 2019 (MAI Act).

The Statement of Intent for 2026­27 has been prepared in accordance with section 61 of the Financial Management Act 1996 (FMA).

The responsible Minister, Rachel Stephen-Smith MLA, was consulted during the preparation of the Statement of Intent.

The Statement of Intent, which focuses on the 2026­27 Budget year, has been developed in the context of a four year forward planning horizon to be incorporated, as far as practicable, into the MAI Commission’s strategic and planning processes.

The MAI Commission’s 2026­27 Statement of Intent has been agreed between:

| Chris Steel MLATreasurer |   | Rachel Stephen-Smith MLAMinister for Finance |

<a id="motor-accident-injuries-commission"></a>

## MOTOR ACCIDENT INJURIES COMMISSION


<a id="introduction"></a>

### Introduction

The MAI Commission is a Territory authority established under the Motor Accident Injuries Act 2019 (MAI Act). The MAI Commission started operations on the commencement of the MAI Act on 1 February 2020.

The Statement of Intent for the MAI Commission, including strategies, key performance indicators and priorities, has been developed by the MAI Commission in accordance with the FMA and the MAI Act.


<a id="purpose-9"></a>

### Purpose

The MAI Commission is established by section 22 of the MAI Act and is headed by the MAI Commissioner. The MAI Commission’s functions are detailed in section 25 of the MAI Act. Its responsibilities include:

regulating the licensing of insurers under the MAI Scheme;

reviewing premiums to ensure they fully fund the present and likely future costs of the scheme but are not excessive;

monitoring insurers’ compliance with their obligations under the Act;

providing information to the public about the MAI Scheme;

managing complaints about the market practices of licensed insurers and the handling practices of insurers under the MAI Scheme;

issuing, monitoring and reviewing the MAI guidelines and other statutory instruments under the Act; and

monitoring and advising the Minister about the administration, efficiency and effectiveness of the MAI Scheme.

The Commission does not have powers to change a decision of a MAI insurer regarding a defined benefits application or a common law claim or exercise any discretion in relation to the application of the MAI legislation.

The functions of the MAI Commission are funded by a levy applied on every motor vehicle registration on a per annum basis.

Nature and Scope of Activities

General activities


<a id="mai-scheme-monitoring-and-insurer-compliance"></a>

##### MAI Scheme monitoring and insurer compliance

The MAI Commission actively monitors the operation of the MAI Scheme using various tools and sources of information. Compliance activities include the analysis of the applications / claims data and on-site audits, together with the follow-up of issues identified through the yearly self-assessment review, reportable conduct notifications and complaints received.

Periodic meetings are held with the Insurance Council of Australia, MAI insurers, the ACT Nominal Defendant, the Defined Benefits Information Service and the authorised Independent Medical Examiner Providers. The Commission also meets on an as needs basis with the ACT Civil and Administrative Tribunal and the legal profession.

The Commission receives feedback from the community regarding the MAI Scheme through the MAI Commission website at www.treasury.act.gov.au/maic, and through direct enquiries (both oral and written correspondence).


<a id="defined-benefits-information-service-and-other-activities"></a>

##### Defined Benefits Information Service and other activities

The Defined Benefits Information Service or DBIS assists the public with information in relation to available benefits for injured people, the application process and how to navigate the scheme. It is a free service funded by the Commission. The DBIS is currently provided by Care Inc, a Canberra based community organisation, through an open procurement process. Care Inc has since established a Social Worker position within their team to provide additional support for vulnerable road users.

The MAI Commission’s website (www.treasury.act.gov.au/maic) provides information about the MAI Scheme. The Commission has been undertaking and will continue to undertake other outreach activities to inform the community and service providers about the MAI Scheme.


<a id="premiums-and-viability-of-the-mai-scheme"></a>

##### Premiums and viability of the MAI Scheme

The MAI Commission is responsible for the approval of premiums proposed by MAI insurers. As part of this role, the Commission reviews the premium filings to determine if they are in accordance with the MAI Act and Premium Guidelines to ensure that the premium fully funds the insurer’s present and likely future liability and is not excessive. This includes considering the advice of the scheme actuary.

The MAI Commission publishes the applicable MAI premiums for all vehicle classes on the MAI Commission’s website see www.treasury.act.gov.au/maic.

The MAI Commission provides targeted funding for various road safety initiatives to promote public awareness of the causes of motor accidents, and ways of reducing and mitigating the impact of motor accidents. A reduction in accidents assists in lowering MAI premiums.


<a id="maintaining-claims-statistics-for-the-mai-scheme"></a>

##### Maintaining claims statistics for the MAI Scheme

The MAI Commission manages an ICT system to assist with data collection and reporting requirements. The ICT system incorporates the MAI Scheme data on a shared platform to provide enhanced and systematic data capture and reporting capabilities. It assists the Commission in monitoring compliance as well as analysing how the scheme is performing relative to its aims.

The data are collected from licensed MAI insurers and the Nominal Defendant at regular intervals. Data are also being collected from lawyers who provide certain types of legal services in relation to the MAI Scheme, through a lawyer portal link that is available on the MAI Commission’s website.

Key MAI Scheme statistics are published quarterly on the website – see the Scheme Knowledge Centre at www.treasury.act.gov.au/maic.


<a id="three-year-review-of-the-mai-scheme"></a>

##### Three Year Review of the MAI Scheme

The MAI Act is required to undergo a review every three years as set out in the legislation. The MAI Commission provides support to Treasury and the Minister for Finance in undertaking the review. This includes data relevant to the operations of the scheme.

Risks

Financial risks include adequacy of funding for the MAI Commission’s activities. A levy of $14 per annum is collected from every motor accident injury insurance policy during vehicle registration to fund the MAI Commission. The MAI Commission’s revenues are thus predominantly dependent upon the number of new registrations and registration renewals.

The MAI Commission has developed a risk management plan to identify and address risks and will review and update this plan as part of its ongoing business processes.

2026-27 Priorities and Next Three Financial Years

Priorities in 2026-27 and over the next three financial years relate to the functions of the MAI Commission and include:

facilitating the 2026 three-year review of the scheme;

issuing additional guidelines and regulations under the MAI Act, as required;

actively monitoring and supervising the conduct of MAI insurers;

assessing MAI premium filings and monitoring premium processes;

maintaining an ICT system for the scheme’s data requirements;

monitoring the scheme’s performance;

reporting on the scheme’s statistics; and

contributing to targeted road safety initiatives that assist in reducing motor accidents, personal injuries and mitigating their impact.

Estimated Employment Levels

In accordance with section 24 of the MAI Act, the Executive Branch Manager, Insurance Branch, Economic and Financial Group, Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate (CMTEDD) has been appointed as MAI Commissioner. The Insurance Branch provides a supporting role to the MAI Commission by providing staff to carry out the Commission’s functions.

Roles and responsibilities of the MAI Commission are delegated by the MAI Commissioner to officers of CMTEDD. The MAI Commission does not employ any staff.

The MAI Commission reimburses the Insurance Branch for the salary and superannuation expenses associated with the staff allocated to carrying out the MAI Commission’s functions.

Key Performance Indicators for 2026-27 to 2029-30

The above should be read in conjunction with the accompanying notes.

Note(s):

A full de novo premium filing is one in which all evidence in support of a filed premium including assumptions and statistical data, are considered anew.  All of the premium filings are assessed and approved in accordance with the MAI Act.

A premium filing is a report provided by a MAI insurer to the MAI Commissioner containing a range of information in support of its MAI premium submissions.  Consistent with the MAI Act, each filing is assessed by the scheme actuary to ensure the MAI insurance Scheme remains fully funded and that the premium is not excessive.  The MAI Commissioner must then approve or reject the premium, and no later than six weeks after the day of receiving the premium application from the licensed insurer, advise the insurer about the decision and the reasons for the decision.  Premiums can be rejected if they will not fully fund the present and likely future liabilities of the insurer; or the premiums are excessive; or the premium does not comply with the MAI premium guidelines.

An actuarial assessment is conducted in respect of each premium filing to ensure each met the fully funded test, that is, the premium meets the present and likely future liabilities of the insurer under the MAI Scheme and that the premium is not excessive.  Consistent with the streamlining arrangements, the MAI Commissioner approves partial premium filings if the change in premiums is within the permitted actuarially set bands, and is equal to or above the agreed minimum amount.

The MAI Commission publishes the new MAI premiums for all vehicle classes on the MAI Commission’s website at www.treasury.act.gov.au/maic on the public release date. The ‘public release date’ is the date from which motorists are able to electronically renew their MAI policies using the new premiums on the Access Canberra website. This date is stated in the letter from the MAI Commission to the insurer approving their premium filing.

The MAI Commission contributes a minimum of $50,000 towards road safety strategies targeted at supporting and promoting the prevention of motor accidents and the safe use of motor vehicles, with the aim of reducing the number and/or severity of injuries arising from these accidents (consistent with the pertinent MAI Commission’s objective under the MAI Act).

Reports are published within six weeks after the end of each quarter.  All scheme statistics reports are published on the MAI Commission website – see the Scheme Knowledge Centre at www.treasury.act.gov.au/maic.

Responses to queries directed to the MAI Commission within ten working days of receipt of the queries apply in cases where the MAI Commission does not need to liaise with another area of government or external entity.  Queries do not include responses received as part of a consultation process.  Also refers to written correspondence only, not phone calls.

Responses to complaints directed to the MAI Commission within twenty working days of receipt of all the details of the complaint, including any clarifications and data from the complainant and/or insurers or another external entity required by the MAI Commission to be able to review issues with an insurer’s handling of the claim.

If the MAI Commission has previously advised a complainant that it will not respond to any further complaints that are considered unreasonable complaint conduct, then subsequent complaints of this type from the complainant will not be regarded as a complaint for the purposes of this indicator. Examples of unreasonable complaint conduct are unsubstantiated complaints and / or complaints restating issues with no new evidence provided. Complaints do not include responses received as part of a consultation process.  Also refers to written correspondence only, not phone calls.  This indicator does not apply to a complaint relating to multiple injured people as such complaints are likely to require more liaison with insurers or other service providers and take longer to finalise.

The MAI Commission can review the decision-making process of an insurer but cannot change the insurer’s actual decision.  An injured person has review avenues under the MAI Scheme in relation to insurer decisions, such as requesting an internal review or lodging an external review application with the ACT Civil and Administrative Tribunal.

The legislation requires the Minister to undertake a review. The Commission assists the reviewers with scheme data, information on its activities and facilitates any input by the scheme actuary.

Assessment of Performance Against 2025-26 Objectives

The MAI Commission’s performance against its objectives and targets from 1 July 2025 to 30 June 2026 included in its 2025-26 Statement of Intent shows that the MAI Commission is expected to meet all its targets. Further non-financial and performance outcomes are detailed below.

MAI Premium filings are reviewed by the scheme actuary and are approved in accordance with the MAI Act including fully funding the scheme

The MAI Commissioner is required to approve or reject a premium application under section 319 of the MAI Act. Under section 318, there are two key grounds on which the MAI Commissioner is permitted to reject a premium filing: the premiums applied for by MAI insurers are too low (the fully funded test); or are too high (the excessive premium test). Consistent with premium streamlining arrangements, the MAI Commissioner approves premium partial filings if the change in premiums is within the permitted set bands and above the agreed minimum amount.

De novo filings from all four MAI insurers and two partial filings were received during the year to date to 30 April 2026. All premium filings were reviewed by the scheme actuary to ensure they met the fully funded test (the scheme can pay out all present and future liabilities) and not excessive test. The scheme actuary considered that all insurers’ premiums met these tests. The premium filings were assessed by the Commission and approved in accordance with the MAI Act.

Insurer MAI premiums for all vehicle classes are published on the MAI Commission’s website

All insurer MAI premiums were published on the MAI website within the target timeframe as at 30 April 2026.

Promote public awareness of the causes of motor accidents through funding measures directed at reducing causes of motor vehicle accidents

The MAI Commission is expected to contribute $127,000 (Excl GST) in 2025-26 towards road safety strategies with initiatives directed at supporting and promoting the prevention of motor accidents and the safe use of motor vehicles. The initiatives aimed to reduce the number and/or severity of injuries arising from motor accidents (consistent with the relevant MAI Commission’s objective under the MAI Act). During the year to date 30 April 2026, the MAI Commission provided the following funding:

$38,000 for the ‘Stop it or Cop it’ drink and drug driving campaign;

$80,000 for the ‘Always on’ campaign for digital road safety signage at petrol stations; and

$6,000 for an extension of the Dangerous Driving Initiative for targeted school facilitation.

Reporting on the scheme’s statistics

The report for each quarter in the year to date to 31 March 2026 was published on the MAI Commission website within target timeframes.

Queries handling within ten working days of receipt of the query

All queries received by the MAI Commission in the year to date to 30 April 2026 were responded to within ten working days of receipt.

Complaints handling within 20 working days of receipt of all the required information.

The MAI Commission did not receive any complaints in the year to date to 30 April 2026.

Monitoring and Reporting

The MAI Commission will satisfy the requirements of the Chief Minister’s Annual Reports Directions.

The MAI Commission’s Annual Report will, amongst other things, report against the requirements of this Statement of Intent.

The FMA authorises the Treasurer to obtain financial and other statements from the MAI Commission for a stated period including annual, quarterly and monthly reporting.

Monthly Reporting

The MAI Commission prepares monthly financial reports for internal scrutiny and management.  These reports include tables and variance analysis for:

an Operating Statement;

a Balance Sheet; and

a Statement of Cash Flows.

Annual Reporting

As part of preparations for end of year reporting CMTEDD will advise the dates when the following documents are required by the Audit Office and to CMTEDD.  These reports include:

certified financial statements;

management discussion and analysis;

a full and accurate set of audited financial records for the preceding financial year in the form requested; and

consolidation packs relating to the annual financial statements, draft and final.

Financial Arrangements

The MAI Commission is a self-funded statutory authority.

The Motor Accident Levy (MA Levy) of $14 per annum is payable in relation to MAI policies issued with effect from 1 July 2023, except for veteran, vintage and historic (VVH) registration scheme vehicles and modified VVH vehicles that have a $3.50 levy per annum per MAI policy. The levy applies for a full year policy and is pro-rated for shorter registration periods.

This levy has been estimated to fully fund the MAI Commission.

Financial statements

an Operating Statement;

a Balance Sheet;

a Statement of Changes in Equity; and

a Statement of Cash Flows.

## OFFICE OF THE WORK HEALTH AND SAFETY COMMISSIONER – STATEMENT OF INTENT

The Office of the Work Health and Safety Commissioner (WorkSafe ACT) is a Territory Authority established under the Work Health and Safety Act 2011.

The Statement of Intent for 2026-27 has been prepared in accordance with Section 61 of the Financial Management Act 1996.

The responsible Minister, Michael Pettersson MLA, was consulted during the preparation of the Statement of Intent.

The Statement of Intent, which focuses on the 2026-27 Budget year, has been developed in the context of a four year forward planning horizon to be incorporated, as far as practicable, into WorkSafe ACT’s strategic and business planning processes.

WorkSafe ACT’s 2026-27 Statement of Intent has been agreed between:

<a id="office-of-the-work-health-and-safety-commissioner"></a>

## OFFICE OF THE WORK HEALTH AND SAFETY COMMISSIONER


<a id="purpose-10"></a>

### Purpose

The Work Health and Safety (WHS) Commissioner is appointed the Regulator under the Work Health and Safety Act 2011 (the Act). The Office of the WHS Commissioner is an independent entity established on 1 July 2020 to support the Regulator in administering the ACT’s WHS and associated legislation. Together, the Regulator and Office of the WHS Commissioner comprise WorkSafe ACT.

The purpose of WorkSafe ACT is to secure the highest level of safety for all workers in all workplaces through fair and firm regulatory activities. WorkSafe ACT also has regulatory responsibility for workers’ compensation, workplace privacy, labour hire licensing, long service leave and related laws in the ACT.

WorkSafe ACT achieves its purpose through a combination of compliance and enforcement activities and supporting awareness of obligations though engagement. This Statement outlines the strategic priorities, objectives and indicators for the 2026-27 financial year and next three financial years and reports against the accountability indicators for the 2025-26 financial year.


<a id="nature-and-scope-of-activities-6"></a>

### Nature and Scope of Activities

As the regulator for WHS and related laws, WorkSafe ACT is responsible for ensuring that persons conducting a business or undertaking (PCBUs) are meeting their duties and keeping their workplaces safe and healthy. The nature and scope of WorkSafe ACT’s activities are guided by the Strategic Plan 2025-2029 and the Compliance and Enforcement Policy 2025-2029.

WorkSafe ACT’s activities include:

monitoring and enforcing compliance with WHS and related legislation

conducting investigations into serious breaches

initiating charges and carrying out prosecutions for offences under the WHS and related legislation when warranted, noting that Court proceedings are the highest form of enforcement activity undertaken by the Regulator

engaging with PCBUs, workers, industry, unions and the broader community to provide information on matters relating to WHS

carrying out certain licensing functions under Workers’ Compensation, Dangerous Substances, and Labour Hire Licensing legislation; and

advising and reporting to the Minister on the operation and effectiveness of the WHS Act


<a id="risks-4"></a>

###### Risks

In delivering WorkSafe ACT’s objectives, the following risks have been identified and mitigation measures applied:

failure to effectively deliver compliance and enforcement activities reduces WorkSafe ACT’s ability to achieve regulatory outcomes, resulting in non-compliance with legislation and increased risk to worker health and safety: mitigated through a full and considered range of measures including compliance and enforcement policies supported by  Standard Operating Procedures, a Quality Management Program, strategic and operational planning processes, workforce capability development programs, data collection and analysis, reporting,  as well as governance and oversight through leadership and committees

internal WHS incidents, risk and hazard (physical and psychosocial) management: mitigated through WorkSafe ACT’s Work Health and Safety Management Plan (WHSMP), which is used to embed a strong WHS culture and to manage health and safety risks in our workplace to minimise injuries and illnesses as a priority. An annual review of the WHSMP includes consideration of risk management, incident reporting and management, health monitoring, consultation, and WHS awareness and training

poor stakeholder relationships leading to lack of engagement and/or collaboration with industry, government, unions, health and safety representatives, duty holders, workers and the broader community resulting in reputational damage due to loss of confidence and trust: mitigated through external communications, engagement events, regular meetings with stakeholders, and awareness raising activities

lack of appropriate governance affecting decision-making: mitigated through the Governance Framework, Audit & Risk Committee, Senior Leadership Team, and Case Management Panel with a focus on strengthening and ensuring consistent regulatory decision-making, clarifying accountabilities and supporting continuous improvement of business policies, processes, practices, and systems

major, unexpected and disruptive incidents (including disasters): mitigated through WorkSafe ACT’s Business Continuity Plan, which once activated, sets out key processes and arrangements to be implemented in response to business interruption events

limitations of the current WHS prosecution model impacting on WorkSafe ACT’s ability to prosecute duty holders: mitigated through a strong investigations and legal policy team, development of a dedicated in-house prosecution function through recruitment of a qualified independent prosecutor, who will improve the timeliness and commitment to prosecute serious offences and repeat offenders

inability to identify and respond to emerging workplace hazards and changes in work practices in a timely manner: mitigated through effective information gathering including anonymous reporting of complaints or concerns, data analysis, enabling targeted strategies for at-risk workers, industries, and addressing priority diseases, conditions, hazards and risks

inadequate information management and the failure to effectively use data to inform better practice: mitigated through ongoing investment in business systems and processes to embed contemporary technologies and practices that support a strong data analytics and evidence building capability which informs targeted compliance programs and reporting

poor implementation of legislative or regulatory change due to external factors: mitigated through a strong relationship with key ACT Government stakeholders including Work Safety Group (Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate) which has responsibility for advising, developing and maintaining relevant legislation, which WorkSafe ACT implements and regulates; and

WorkSafe ACT failing to meet the expectations of the Minister provided in the annual Statement of Expectations: mitigated through considered project planning, quarterly reporting to the WHS Council, monthly meetings with the Minister, monitoring response timeframes and the allocation of dedicated resources to effectively manage business requirements and track progress against identified projects.


<a id="priorities-for-2026-27-and-next-three-financial-years"></a>

### Priorities for 2026-27 and Next Three Financial Years

Following a period of significant transformation and growth since its establishment as an independent regulator in 2020, WorkSafe ACT continues to mature as an agency focused on strengthening its adaptability to meet the evolving safety needs of the community. We are achieving this through continuous improvement of internal capacity and capability, including enhancing our ability to respond to changes in WHS practices and legislation.

WorkSafe ACT is committed to leading by example as a healthy and safe workplace, enabling its workers to make important and meaningful contributions to the community.

Building on recent progress and supported by a growing body of data and intelligence, WorkSafe ACT will continue to be guided by its Strategic Plan 2025-2029. WorkSafe ACT will ensure the continued efficient and effective use of our resources to meet our regulatory responsibilities. By continuing to strengthen our capability development, targeted recruitment activities and ongoing system updates, we ensure our workforce has the resources to target high-risk hazards and industries and apply the appropriate enforcement tools required to meet the safety needs of ACT workplaces.

WorkSafe ACT’s Compliance and Enforcement Policy 2025-2029 clearly sets out the approach we take to our regulatory activities, including the considerations that will be applied when deciding on appropriate enforcement measures. This approach is complemented by the recently developed Workers’ Compensation Compliance and Enforcement Policy, which outlines how WorkSafe ACT promotes and enforces compliance with workers’ compensation legislation through proportionate, risk-based regulation. As part of our commitment to being a strong regulator, our investigations and legal policy team continues to review and refine its processes, enabling a strong focus on investigations and prosecutions. WHS prosecutions have been identified as an effective enforcement tool, as they both highlight and deter unsafe practices, reinforce the importance of meeting safety duties, and raise awareness of safety matters within the community. Prosecutions are prioritised within our broader compliance and enforcement activities to ensure non-compliance is addressed in a firm and proportionate manner, particularly for serious offences and repeat offenders.

WorkSafe ACT’s recently established internal Quality Management Program (the Program) represents a strategic commitment to continuous improvement, operational excellence, and the effective delivery of our legislative responsibilities. It provides a robust framework for assessing strengths, identifying systemic risks, and implementing evidence-based enhancements to our compliance and enforcement functions. The Program was developed in alignment with internationally recognised standards, including ISO 9001:2015 and AS/NZS ISO 19011:2019.

Our ongoing inspector training, refinement of standard operating procedures, and capability development initiatives, together with a recent internal restructure, will support inspectors to consistently deliver high-quality regulatory services across all industries and circumstances. This will also ensure that inspectors can apply the appropriate level of scrutiny to identify complex or high‑risk cases and escalate them for investigation and prosecution where warranted. This approach supports our ongoing shift from an educative approach to a more targeted, strict enforcement‑focused model for serious offences and repeat offenders.

WorkSafe ACT’s proactive compliance and enforcement activities will continue to be driven by data analysis and intelligence. While our overall regulatory approach in 2026-27 will prioritise responding to incidents, complaints and notifications, the construction industry (including commercial, civil and residential construction) and the ACT Public Service will each be a key focus of targeted proactive efforts.

For the construction industry, our campaign strategies have been adapted and extended into 2026‑27. Key initiatives include focusing on repeat offenders to ensure enforcement is targeted to the workplaces that have demonstrated an ongoing lack of compliance.

The ACT Public Service is the largest business regulated by WorkSafe ACT and represents a diverse workforce with a wide range of responsibilities. We will work to strengthen WHS knowledge and compliance in this sector and address emerging issues through proactive campaigns to inspect current WHS systems, identifying areas for improvement, and promoting awareness of statutory obligations.

Psychosocial hazards will continue to be a significant area of regulatory focus across multiple industries, reflecting their persistent and emerging impact on worker health and safety in workplaces. This includes work-related violence and aggression, sexual harassment, and sexual assault incidents.

Using notifiable incident data relating to sexual assault, WorkSafe ACT will undertake targeted proactive campaigns to inform, engage, and support PCBUs to ensure they have appropriate systems, policies, processes, and procedures in place to eliminate so far as is reasonably practicable incidents of sexual assault in workplaces.

Engagement with stakeholders to improve workplace maturity in identifying and managing persistent and emerging WHS issues is an ongoing priority for WorkSafe ACT. To support improved compliance and effective risk management across workplaces, our activities ensure we provide relevant and timely information to our stakeholders. These include:

targeted and focused stakeholder engagement with workers and employers in priority industries, with a focus on vulnerable workers and providing information for culturally and linguistically diverse people

improving WorkSafe ACT’s website content and structure to ensure the information is accessible, relevant and up to date

providing clearer guidance on identifying and managing physical and psychosocial risks in the workplace

providing relevant updates on legislative changes and new or updated Codes of Practice, particularly relating to psychosocial hazards; and

developing new resources to support stakeholders, including:

information for workers and their families on workers’ compensation and relevant legal matters in the event of a serious injury or fatality occurring

guidance for managing risks in the supply and contractual chain

information on emergency plans for natural disasters

clarification on duties under the Workplace Privacy Act 2011, focusing on hybrid work arrangements

a small business information toolkit, and

information to support health and safety representatives (HSRs) and strengthen their capacity.

<a id="strategic-objectives-and-indicators-4"></a>

### Strategic Objectives and Indicators

WorkSafe ACT’s activities are guided by the Strategic Plan 2025-2029, consistent with the Australian Work Health and Safety Strategy 2023-2033 (Australian Strategy) which sets an ambitious vision for WHS outcomes in Australia and is focused on reducing worker fatalities, injuries and illnesses. The Australian Strategy identifies the following key enablers in working towards achieving key targets:

embed good WHS practice in all work, across all industries, cohorts, and hazards

innovate and deepen knowledge of WHS to broaden understanding; and

collaborate collectively and cooperatively to respond to WHS challenges.

WorkSafe ACT’s three overarching Strategies and their objectives for 2025-2029 are:

Timely and effective compliance and enforcement activities with a focus on investigations and prosecutions:

secure the highest level of compliance with WHS legislation through targeted enforcement activities. Focusing on specific priority industries ensures fair and firm enforcement. Where breaches are identified, swift action will be taken to enforce compliance and protect workers.

Engagement with stakeholders to increase workplace maturity in identifying and managing persistent and emerging WHS issues:

foster collaborative relationships with stakeholders to facilitate knowledge sharing and encourage a strong safety culture and climate across all workplaces. Work with industry, unions, HSRs, duty holders, workers and the broader community to increase the maturity of safety systems.

WorkSafe ACT is an adaptable and effective regulator:

enhance our adaptability as a regulator to meet the growing safety needs of the community through continuous improvement of our internal capacity and capability. WorkSafe ACT is also dedicated to managing and auditing risks within the agency.

<a id="output-11-regulatory-compliance-and-enforcement"></a>

##### Output 1.1 Regulatory compliance and enforcement

The aim of WorkSafe ACT’s compliance and enforcement activity is to prevent work-related deaths, injuries and diseases and to protect property and the environment from the damage presented by hazards associated with work, workplaces and dangerous substances, by securing the highest possible level of compliance with legislation.

WorkSafe ACT’s regulatory approach and decision-making process is detailed in its Compliance and Enforcement Policy 2025-2029. Integral to WorkSafe ACT’s approach is the recognition that:

duty holders in control of work or premises that pose risks to health and safety are in the best position to eliminate or minimise these risks

real and sustainable improvement in managing risks to health and safety is achieved through the active involvement of all duty holders and workers in the processes of hazard identification, risk assessment and management and the review of control measures; and

where warranted, ensure that enforcement by way of prosecutions of offences under the WHS laws are carried out and that such actions are both an effective deterrent to unsafe practices and a means of maximising work health and safety practices.

Measures the percentage of major investigations completed (referred for enforcement/regulatory action) within 12 months of the investigation’s commencement. The major investigations relevant to this indicator are those completed (not commenced) in a financial year.

<a id="monitoring-and-reporting-5"></a>

### Monitoring and Reporting

WorkSafe ACT will satisfy the requirements of the Chief Minister’s Annual Reports Directions. The WorkSafe ACT Annual Report will, amongst other things, report against the requirements of this Statement of Intent.

The Financial Management Act 1996 authorises the Treasurer to obtain financial and other statements from WorkSafe ACT for a stated period including annual reporting.


<a id="annual-reporting-5"></a>

###### Annual Reporting

As part of preparations for end of year reporting, CMTEDD will advise the dates when the following documents are required by CMTEDD and at the Auditor-General's Office:

certified financial statements;

management discussion and analysis;

a full and accurate set of audited financial records for the preceding financial year in the form requested; and

consolidation packs relating to the annual financial statements, draft and final.


<a id="financial-arrangements-5"></a>

### Financial Arrangements

A majority of WorkSafe ACT’s budgeted operating revenue is received as an appropriation based on the estimated expenditure for the period.

<a id="public-sector-workers-compensation-fund"></a>

## PUBLIC SECTOR WORKERS COMPENSATION FUND


<a id="purpose-11"></a>

### Purpose

The Public Sector Workers Compensation Fund (PSWCF or PSWC Fund) is established by the Public Sector Workers Compensation Fund Act 2018 (PSWCFA) and commenced operations on 1 March 2019.

The PSWCFA provides a financial and prudential governance framework to support the Territory’s workers’ compensation self-insurance arrangements for public sector employees, which are delivered under a licence issued by the Safety Rehabilitation and Compensation Commission in accordance with the Safety Rehabilitation and Compensation Act 1988 (SRC Act).

The PSWCF has been designed to ensure the effective management of the Territory’s workers’ compensation assets and features governance and management arrangements in relation to those assets.  The PSWCF is integral to the Territory’s compliance with its self-insurance licence.

The PSWCF is administered by the Public Sector Workers Compensation Commissioner (PSWC Commissioner) who is responsible for:

managing the PSWCF;

advising the Minister about the administration, efficiency and effectiveness of the PSWC fund; and

in relation to a licence granted to the Territory under section 103 of the SRC Act:

managing the Territory’s liability under the SRC Act; and

managing claims under the SRC Act.

The PSWCF is funded by the Territory, including from annual contributions apportioned by the PSWC Commissioner amongst Territory agencies (directorates and entities).  Assets are invested, with earnings retained to help meet workers’ compensation costs.

2026-27 Priorities

Ongoing strategic and operational priorities for the PSWCF include:

conducting and supporting initiatives and activities to prevent and reduce the risk of injury to employees of the Territory;

developing and deploying best practice health, wellbeing and return to work strategies, policies and interventions for the ACT public sector;

ensuring the effective and efficient management of new and existing ACT public sector workers’ compensation claims;

ensuring the effective and efficient management of PSWCF assets in accordance with the PSWCF investment guidelines; and

continuously improving injured worker and directorate satisfaction with workers’ compensation insurance and rehabilitation services.

The PSWCF will also pursue the following priorities in 2026­27:

ensuring the effective and efficient implementation of the AASB 17 Insurance Contracts accounting standard from 1 July 2026;

ensuring the effective and efficient management of the self-insurance licence renewal by February 2027; and

ensuring the effective and efficient implementation of the new TPA agreement from March 2027.

Estimated Employment Levels

The functions of the PSWCF are performed by officers of the Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate (CMTEDD) and those employees are included in CMTEDD’s FTE levels.  The PSWCF may reimburse CMTEDD for expenses associated with the staff allocated to carry out PSWCF functions.

Strategic Objectives and Indicators

Strategic Objective 1

The PSWCF will support the achievement of the harm prevention and return to work objective

Strategic Indicator 1: To achieve superior health and return to work outcomes for ACT Public Sector workers who are injured in the course of their work.

Strategic Objective 2

The PSWCF will fund the workers’ compensation liabilities of the ACT Public Sector

Strategic Indicator 2: To achieve and maintain a fully funded scheme.

Strategic Objective 3

The PSWCF will support the achievement of the investment return objective

Strategic Indicator 3: To achieve a long-term annualised return of CPI plus 1.5 per cent to 2.5 per cent.

Output Classes (Territorial)

EBT Class 1 (EBT 1.1): Public Sector Workers Compensation Fund

The output involves the management of ACT public sector workers’ compensation self-insurance, in accordance with the SRC Act.  This includes managing the PSWC Fund and providing associated claim management and vocational rehabilitation services.

The key outputs to be delivered in 2026­27 include:

delivering best practice health, wellbeing and return to work strategies, policies and interventions; and

continuously improving workers’ compensation claim management service standards.

<a id="accountability-indicators-6"></a>

### Accountability Indicators

EBT Class 1 (EBT 1.1): Public Sector Workers Compensation Fund


**Table 2: Accountability Indicators EBT 1.1**


|   | 2025­26Targets | 2025­26Estimated Outcome | 2026­27Targets |
|---|---|---|---|
| Reduce the ACT public sector incidence of serious workplace injury | 12.7 | 12.1 | 12.4 |
| Achieve a conformance rating of 85 per cent or higher in the annual audit of the ACT workers compensation self-insurance rehabilitation management system | ≥85% | 100% | ≥85% |
| Maintain a PSWC Fund asset to liability ratio greater than or equal to 100 per cent | ≥100% | 192% | ≥100% |
| Achieve a conformance rating of 85 per cent or higher in the annual audit of the ACT workers compensation self-insurance claims management system | ≥85% | 86% | ≥85% |

Note(s):

This accountability indicator measures the number of ACT public servant workers’ compensation claims resulting in absence from the workplace of one week or more, per 1,000 employees. The measurement methodology aligns with the ACT Government’s self-insurance licence and the Safety, Rehabilitation and Compensation Commission’s Licensee Compliance and Performance model. The 2026­27 target aims to achieve 10 per cent reduction in the incidence rate of serious workplace injuries over five years (2024-25 to 2028-29). This target level of improvement is informed by national Safe Work Australia work injury reduction targets.

It is a condition of the ACT Government’s self-insurance licence that the Territory maintain a rehabilitation management system that complies with Commonwealth guidelines and that conformance be audited annually. The target was exceeded in 2025­26, with the outcomes of a self-administered independent audit conducted in year 7 of the self-insurance licence recording 100 per cent compliance with the relevant rehabilitation standards and associated audit criteria.

This accountability indicator aligns with requirements under the Public Sector Workers Compensation Fund (Investment and Funding Ratio) Management Guidelines 2019 (No 2). An asset to liability ratio greater than 100 per cent indicates that the PSWCF has sufficient assets to be able to cover the expected lifetime costs of public sector workers’ compensation claims and other liabilities it owes. The 2025­26 estimated outcome ratio of total assets to total liabilities was 192 per cent.

It is a condition of the ACT Government’s self-insurance licence that the Territory maintains a claims management system that complies with Commonwealth guidelines and that conformance be audited annually. The target was met in 2025­26, with the outcomes of a self-administered independent audit conducted in year 7 of the self-insurance licence recording 86 per cent compliance with the relevant claims management standards and associated audit criteria.

Financial Statements – Territorial

Transition to AASB 17 Insurance Contracts

Currently the PSWCF applies AASB 1058 Income for Not-for-Profit Entities to recognise underwriting income from contributions paid into the PSWCF and AASB 137 Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets to value outstanding claims provisions and associated claims expenses. From 1 July 2026 the PSWCF will adopt AASB 17 Insurance Contracts. The application of AASB 17 changes the measurement, recognition and disclosure of the underwriting revenue and expenses, the assets associated with claims related expected recoveries and the liabilities associated with outstanding claims provisions.

The 2026­27 Budget is the first budget prepared reflecting the 2026­27 and forward years financial estimates under AASB 17.

The PSWCF will adopt the full retrospective approach on transition. However, the 2026­27 Budget does not restate prior year comparative estimates for 2025­26. The accounting impact arising on transition is reflected as an adjustment to the opening accumulated funds as at 1 July 2026 in the PSWCF’s Statement of Changes in Equity on Behalf of the Territory.

While the 2026­27 and forward years estimates are calculated based on the requirements of AASB 17, the presentation continues to follow the existing budget framework. Accordingly, the classification and presentation of insurance contracts in the 2026­27 Budget may differ from the 2026­27 financial statements, which will be prepared under AASB 17. As a result, the 2026­27 Budget presented in the 2026­27 financial statements will be different to the existing budget framework. There is no impact on the overall financial estimates because of this presentational difference.

The transition to AASB 17 does not impact the operation of the PSWCF.

Notes to the Territorial Budget Statements

Significant variations are as follows:

Statement of Income and Expenses on behalf of the Territory

sale of goods and services from contracts with customers:

the increase of $14.660 million in the 2026­27 Budget from the 2025­26 estimated outcome is attributed to an overall increase in the workers' compensation rate; and

the 2026­27 Budget and forward years reflect workforce and wages growth expectations.

investment revenue:

the decrease of $4.083 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects the lower than expected distributions on investments with the Territory Banking Account with an estimated total return of 3.01 per cent for 2025­26, which is below the target total return of CPI plus 1.5 per cent and the expected total return of 4.29 per cent for the original budget; and

the increase of $2.522 million in the 2026­27 Budget from the 2025­26 estimated outcome reflects planned investments for 2026­27 and the latest advice from the Territory Banking Account in relation to the target total return of CPI plus 1.5 per cent indicating an expected total return of 4.37 per cent for 2026­27 and the forward years.

interest revenue:

the increase of $7.093 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects higher than expected interest revenue earned attributed to higher than expected net cashflows from operating activities and higher than expected interest rate earned on cash at bank; and

the 2026­27 Budget and forward years reflect expected interest earned on cash at bank based on expected cashflows during each year. While relatively conservative compared to the RBA target cash rate of 4.35 per cent per annum (as of 6 May 2026), an average interest rate earned on cash at bank of about 1.13 to 1.15 per cent per annum is assumed to balance potential lower than expected investment revenue due to short term market volatility.

net gain/(loss) on investments:

with considerable short-term market volatility observed throughout 2025­26, the original budget expectation was maintained and the complement from the estimated total return of 3.01 per cent for 2025­26 reflected in the investment revenue 2025­26 estimated outcome above; and

the increase of $3.277 million in the 2026­27 Budget from the 2025­26 estimated outcome reflects planned investments for 2026­27 and the latest advice from the Territory Banking Account in relation to target total return of CPI plus 1.5 per cent indicating an expected total return of 4.37 per cent for 2026­27 and the forward years.

supplies and services:

the increase of $0.299 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due to costs associated with additional the recovery of CMTEDD staff costs for additional redeployment services provided by CMTEDD Staff to the PSWCF; and

the increase of $0.674 million in the 2026­27 Budget from the 2025­26 estimated outcome reflects anticipated increases in actuarial fees, the self-insurance licence fees, and audit and assurance costs due to indexation and additional service requirements. In addition, the expense associated with recovery of CMTEDD staff costs for services provided to the PSWCF is expected to increase due to the additional redeployment services and the continuation and expansion of the in-house workplace rehabilitation services.

underwriting expenses:

the increase of $19.362 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects higher than expected claims expense based on the latest actuarial advice available and taking into account deteriorations indicated by recent claims experience to 31 December 2025, with claims management expenses remaining largely in line with the original budget expectations; and

the decrease of $7.536 million in the 2026­27 Budget from the 2025­26 estimated outcome reflects claims management expenses increasing in line with the associated with the fees and indexation terms agreed for the TPA plus claims expenses corresponding to no deterioration or improvement in prior year claims (developing in line with actuarial expectations), new claims following expectations underlying the associated 2026­27 workers’ compensation contributions (breakeven assumption) and limited impact from the transition to AASB 17.

the claims management expenses fees for the 2026­27 Budget and forward years were estimated and projected based on the fees and indexation terms agreed for the current TPA agreement second extension to the end of February 2027. Due to the timing of finalising the procurement, the new TPA agreement that will come into effect in March 2027 have not been reflected yet.

total other comprehensive income/(loss): the $43.590 million other comprehensive loss in the 2026­27 Budget reflects the anticipated net impact of the change in accounting policy relating to the full retrospective transition to AASB 17 and the adjustment to the opening accumulated funds as at 1 July 2026 in the Statement of Changes in Equity on Behalf of the Territory.

Statement of Assets and Liabilities on behalf of the Territory

cash and cash equivalents: the increase of $8.456 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects the higher than expected net cash inflows from operating activities mainly attributed to higher than expected interest revenue receipts and lower than expected claims payments offset by lower than expected investment revenue receipts.

investments (non-current):

the increase of $12.479 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects the observed market conditions with investments in 2025­26 following the original budget planned investments; and

the increase of $23.665 million in the 2026­27 Budget from the 2025­26 estimated outcome reflects planned investments and forecast market movements indicated by the latest advice from the Territory Banking Account in relation to the capital component of the expected total return of 4.37 per cent for 2026­27 and the forward years.

receivables (current):

the decrease of $2.149 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget is largely due to distributions receivable on investments with the Territory Banking Account being lower than expected; and

the 2026­27 and the forward years for simplicity forecast a rolling constant expected distributions receivable on investments with the Territory Banking Account and an expected net GST receivable from the ATO reflecting estimates of associated GST cashflows.

claims related recoveries (current and non-current):

the decrease of $0.108 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects updated estimates based on the latest actuarial advice available and considering recent claims experience to 31 December 2025; and

the 2026­27 Budget and forward years assume claims related recoveries will follow gross outstanding claims liabilities and reflect the transition to AASB 17 as of 1 July 2026.

other provisions (current and non-current):

the increase of $32.219 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects expected strengthening in provision for outstanding claims liabilities based on the latest actuarial advice available and taking into account deteriorations indicated by recent claims experience to 31 December 2025; and

the increase of $55.570 million in the 2026­27 Budget from the 2025­26 estimated outcome largely reflects the impact of the transition to AASB 17 measurement with other movements in the 2026­27 Budget and the forward years following the latest available actuarial estimates and expectations for new claims provisions and future claims payments.

payables (current)

the increase of $0.354 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects updates to estimates for accrued expenses including expected TPA service and performance fees; and

the 2026­27 Budget and forward years reflect a rolling constant forecast for simplicity.

other liabilities (current):

the decrease of $0.972 million in the 2025­26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects updates to estimates of end of month allowances for claims costs not yet reimbursed to Territory agencies, PAYG tax withholding not yet remitted to the ATO and reconciling items relating to claims costs paid by the TPA; and

the 2026­27 Budget and forward years reflect a rolling constant forecast for simplicity.

<a id="superannuation-provision-account"></a>

## SUPERANNUATION PROVISION ACCOUNT


<a id="purpose-12"></a>

### Purpose

The Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate (CMTEDD), through the financial operations of the Superannuation Provision Account, assists the Government to effectively manage the defined benefit employer superannuation liabilities of the Territory.

These liabilities are related to past and current ACT employees who are members of the Australian Government’s Commonwealth Superannuation Scheme and Public Sector Superannuation Scheme and Members of the Legislative Assembly Defined Benefit Superannuation Scheme.


<a id="2026-27-priorities-5"></a>

### 2026-27 Priorities

Strategic and operational priorities to be pursued in 2026-27 include:

completing the triennial actuarial valuation review of the Territory’s defined benefit employer superannuation liability using salary and membership data on 30 June 2026;

managing the Investment Plan of the Superannuation Provision Account; and

managing the funding plan to extinguish the unfunded defined benefit superannuation liability.


<a id="estimated-employment-levels-8"></a>

### Estimated Employment Levels

The functions of the Superannuation Provision Account are performed by officers of the Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate (CMTEDD) and those employees are included in CMTEDD’s FTE levels. The Superannuation Provision Account reimburses CMTEDD for the salary and superannuation expenses associated with the staff allocated to carrying out the functions of the Superannuation Provision Account.


<a id="strategic-objectives-and-indicators-5"></a>

### Strategic Objectives and Indicators


<a id="strategic-objective-1-2"></a>

#### Strategic Objective 1

Achievement of Investment Return Objective

To achieve a long-term investment return of CPI + 4.75 per cent per annum.1

Figure 1: Strategic Indicator 1 – Investment Return

Note(s):

This indicator measures the annual Superannuation Provision Account portfolio investment return since the base performance financial year of 1996-97.  The objective is to achieve an investment return of CPI + 4.75 per cent per annum over the long-term, calculated since the base financial year.  Over the past 29 financial years to 30 June 2025 the portfolio has achieved an investment return of CPI + 5.2 per cent per annum, which exceeds the investment return objective.


<a id="strategic-objective-2"></a>

#### Strategic Objective 2

Funding the Defined Benefit Superannuation Liability

To fully fund the defined benefit superannuation liability over the next decade.1

Figure 2: Strategic Indicator 2 – Liability Funding

Note(s):

This indicator measures the percentage of the defined benefit superannuation liabilities funded by financial investment assets. The funding objective is to increase the liability funding percentage over time with 100 per cent coverage over the next decade. The liability funding percentage is calculated by comparing the total value of assets to the total value of liabilities at the end of each financial year. From 30 June 2026 the projected defined benefit superannuation liability valuation is based on a long-term average discount rate assumption of 5 per cent.


<a id="output-classes-territorial-2"></a>

### Output Classes (Territorial)


<a id="ebt-class-1-ebt-11-superannuation-provision-account"></a>

<a id="ebt-11-superannuation-provision-account"></a>

##### EBT 1.1: Superannuation Provision Account

This output involves the management of the Territory’s defined benefit employer superannuation liabilities and financial investment assets.

The key outputs to be delivered in 2026-27 include:

managing the Investment Plan and reporting on the financial investment assets set aside to fund the Territory’s defined benefit employer superannuation liability;

completing the annual superannuation liability valuation review of the Territory’s Commonwealth Superannuation Scheme and Public Sector Superannuation Scheme defined benefit employer superannuation liability;

managing the defined benefit superannuation liability funding plan and emerging cost payments to the Commonwealth Government in respect of the Territory’s employer share of employee retirement benefits;

completing the annual Member Information Statements for the Members of the Legislative Assembly Defined Benefit Superannuation Scheme, with calculation and settlement of benefit determinations as required; and

budgeting and financial reporting on the operations of the Superannuation Provision Account.

<a id="territory-banking-account"></a>

## TERRITORY BANKING ACCOUNT


<a id="purpose-13"></a>

### Purpose

The Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate, through the financial operations of the Territory Banking Account, provides services to the Government including financial asset and liability management.

The Territory Banking Account is established to recognise and manage the Government’s financial investment assets and borrowing liabilities. Revenues on behalf of the Territory are transferred to the Territory Banking Account and fortnightly budget appropriation disbursements are made to agencies from the Territory Banking Account.


<a id="2026-27-priorities-6"></a>

### 2026-27 Priorities

Strategic and operational priorities to be pursued in 2026-27 include:

managing the Territory’s borrowing program;

managing the levels of cash and liquidity;

managing the centralised investment administration platform for Territory financial investment assets; and

managing and reporting on the Government’s Responsible Investment policy.


<a id="estimated-employment-levels-9"></a>

### Estimated Employment Levels

The functions of the Territory Banking Account are performed by officers of the Chief Minister, Treasury and Economic Development Directorate (CMTEDD) and those employees are included in CMTEDD’s FTE levels. The Territory Banking Account reimburses CMTEDD for the salary and superannuation expenses associated with the staff allocated to carrying out the Territory Banking Account functions.


<a id="strategic-objectives-and-indicators-6"></a>

### Strategic Objectives and Indicators


<a id="strategic-objective-1-3"></a>

#### Strategic Objective 1

Management of Cash and Liquidity

The Territory Banking Account is the central public account for the Territory. The aggregate daily cash balance of the Territory Banking Account is managed to provide sufficient cash to support the disbursement of budget appropriation payments to agencies and the settlement and payment of debt obligations of the Government.

The achievement of this objective involves:

the daily monitoring of cash flows and raising funding as required to maintain appropriate levels of liquidity to meet the Government’s financial obligations.


<a id="strategic-objective-2-2"></a>

#### Strategic Objective 2

Management of the Government’s Borrowing Requirements

The activities of the Territory Banking Account include the issuance of debt instruments to fund the Government’s borrowing requirements.

The achievement of this objective involves:

managing the Territory’s A$ debt issuance programme which provides access to funding from the Australian capital markets; and

establishing benchmark bond lines with varying maturity tenors and volumes to manage refinancing risk and improve the liquidity of issued bonds.


<a id="strategic-objective-3"></a>

#### Strategic Objective 3

Centralised Management of the Government’s Financial Investments

To facilitate an efficient cash and investment management structure a centralised and unitised investment administration structure is provided by Treasury that provides several investment options to meet the specific investment objectives of those Directorates and Territory Authorities with financial investment requirements.

The achievement of this objective involves:

the management of the investments in accordance with the Financial Management Act 1996;

investments being made in accordance with the Government’s Responsible Investment Policy and agency investment plans; and

the establishment of investment strategy options with varying (CPI + X%) return target objectives to meet investor risk and return objectives.

<a id="ebt-11-territory-banking-account"></a>

##### EBT 1.1: Territory Banking Account

This output involves the management of the Government’s investment and borrowing activities and the Territory Banking Account as the central account of the Government. The key outputs to be delivered in 2026-27 include:

managing the Territory Banking Account centralised investment platform for Territory financial investment assets;

managing the Government’s Responsible Investment policy framework;

managing the borrowing liabilities of the Territory Banking Account;

raising new Territory borrowings as required;

managing the cash flow and liquidity requirements of the Territory Banking Account; and

budgeting and financial reporting on the operations of the Territory Banking Account.

Accountability Indicator Descriptions and Variance Commentary:

This accountability indicator involves maintaining a positive cash balance of the Territory Banking Account to meet ongoing cash payment obligations.  For performance measurement purposes if the cash balance is not positive at the end of the day, this will not be counted in the result.

This accountability indicator incorporates the monitoring of the investment portfolio to ensure it is not exposed to any prohibited investments, in accordance with the Government’s Responsible Investment policy.  For performance measurement, the actual portfolio direct share holdings will be compared with the prevailing prohibited shares list at the end of each month.  The exposure measure will be the weighted value of any prohibited share investments on the total value of the share portfolio.

This accountability indicator incorporates the exercising of proxy voting rights in accordance with the Government’s Responsible Investment policy.  The target is that more than 95 per cent of all eligible voting items in the year will be cast in relation to the total voting items.  The measure will be the total actual votes cast compared to total eligible voting items.

This accountability indicator incorporates raising new Territory borrowing requirements in accordance with approved borrowing limits and borrowing approvals.  The assessment will be that each borrowing is made in accordance with a borrowing approval.  The reported outcome will be the actual number of conforming borrowing transactions compared to the total borrowing transactions completed.

This accountability indicator incorporates accurate and timely payment of Territory debt interest and principal repayment obligations.  The measure will be the actual number of conforming debt servicing settlement transactions compared with the total number of settlement transactions completed.

Discontinued indicator.  This accountability indicator is discontinued from 2026-27 following a review of all accountability indicators and removing business as usual activity-based indicators.

Discontinued indicator.  This accountability indicator is discontinued from 2026-27 following a review of all accountability indicators and removing business as usual activity-based indicators.

New indicator. This accountability indicator measures the implementation of agency investment plans.

<a id="notes-to-the-territorial-budget-statements-4"></a>

#### Notes to the Territorial Budget Statements

Many of the variations in the Territory Banking Account budget statements are driven by agency activity during and between financial years.

Significant variations are as follows:


<a id="statement-of-income-and-expenses-on-behalf-of-the-territory-4"></a>

##### Statement of Income and Expenses on behalf of the Territory

investment revenue:

the decrease of $51.945 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is related to dividends and distributions from investments which are determined by the level of available distributable income; and

the increase of $50.622 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to dividend and distribution revenue being based on expected distributable income incorporating estimated investments balances and the long-term investment return objectives for the portfolio investment strategies.

interest revenue:

the increase of $40.150 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due to higher interest on cash at bank balances and higher loan interest from Icon Water in relation to inflation-linked loans; and

the increase of $81.798 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to higher cash at bank and investment interest and higher loan interest from Icon Water.

transfer revenue: represents the transfer from agencies of revenue received on behalf of the Territory and includes taxes, fees, fines and grants. Revenue variances are driven by agency activity.

other income:

the decrease of $10.857 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due to lower agencies notional superannuation employer superannuation contributions and other investment revenue; and

the increase of $11.052 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is mainly due to higher agencies notional superannuation employer superannuation contributions and other investment revenue.

net gain/(loss) on investments:

the increase of $7.520 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is determined by the level of investment balances and estimated performance outcome of the underlying asset class sectors; and

the increase of $32.412 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is determined by the estimated level of investment balances and the long-term investment return assumptions for each of the underlying asset class sectors.

payments to ACT Government agencies: represents the transfer of budget appropriation funds to agencies. Variances are driven by agency activity.

borrowing costs:

the increase of $17.636 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due to a higher borrowing requirement and the impact of higher CPI on inflation-linked bonds; and

the increase of $161.666 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome reflects the estimated new borrowing requirements.

other expenses: comprises investment and borrowing administration expenses. Expenses are determined by investment balances and borrowing transactions.

agency investment expenses:

the decrease of $52.555 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is determined by the level of investment balances, estimated performance and the amount of distributable income from each of the underlying asset class sectors.

the increase of $108.438 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is determined by the estimated level of investment balances and the long-term investment return assumptions for each of the underlying asset class sectors.


<a id="statement-of-assets-and-liabilities-on-behalf-of-the-territory-4"></a>

##### Statement of Assets and Liabilities on behalf of the Territory

cash and cash equivalents:

the increase of $288.421 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget reflects a higher cash at bank balance from underlying aggregate cash flow transactions; and

the increase of $1,902.027 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome reflects the estimated operational and liquidity requirements of the Territory Banking Account.

investments:

the increase of $120.366 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is determined by the by the level of investment balances and estimated performance outcome; and

the increase of $618.957 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is determined by the estimated level of investment balances and the long-term investment return assumptions.

receivables (current and non-current) are accrued transfer revenue receivables from agencies and loans provided to Icon Water:

the net decrease of $61.260 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due lower loan receivables from Icon Water and accrued receivables from agencies; and

the net increase of $456.837 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome comprises higher loan receivables from Icon Water and higher accrued receivables from agencies.

borrowings (current and non-current):

the net increase of $330.759 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is mainly due to a higher borrowing requirement than originally assumed; and

the net increase of $3,499.401 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome reflects an increase in net borrowings to meet budget funding requirements.

agency investment deposits:

the increase of $120.366 million in the 2025-26 estimated outcome from the original budget is determined by the by the level of investment balances and estimated performance outcome; and

the increase of $618.956 million in the 2026-27 Budget from the 2025-26 estimated outcome is determined by the estimated level of investment balances and the long-term investment return assumption.

<a id="appendix-a-discontinued-agency-cit-solutions"></a>

## APPENDIX A: DISCONTINUED AGENCY – CIT SOLUTIONS


<a id="purpose-14"></a>

### Purpose

In December 2024, CIT Solutions Pty Ltd (CIT Solutions) undertook a Strategic Review to establish the value proposition of the company and assess the suitability of the current business model. Following the Strategic Review, in March 2025 the company Board recommended, and the CIT Board decided to wind down and close the company. CIT Solutions ceased operations and officially closed in August 2025.

Prior to the closure CIT Solutions was a wholly owned subsidiary of the Canberra Institute of Technology (CIT), established under the Corporations Act in 1988.
